# TO THE READER

The Treatise of the *Way of Perfection* <note>Written by Saint Teresa of Avila (1515–1582) around 1566, this work was originally composed as a guide for her nuns, focusing on prayer and monastic virtue.</note> written by Saint Teresa appeared so useful and so edifying to several pious individuals that they desired it to be translated into our language <note>The "our language" refers to French, the language of this edition.</note>, so that, being printed separately, it could more easily be in the hands of everyone. For even though the doctrine of this great Saint is truly heavenly, and all her writings deserve to be equally revered: nevertheless, there are found in the others, in some places, things so extraordinarily sublime that they seem less suited to the understanding and ordinary piety of the Faithful.

But what is particular about this book, *The Way of Perfection*, is that it appears that the...

ã ij <note>This mark, known as a "signature," was used by early modern printers to help bookbinders assemble the folded sheets of paper in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This preface introduces a French translation of Saint Teresa of Avila’s "The Way of Perfection," explaining that it was published separately to make her spiritual guidance more accessible to a general audience.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, The Way of Perfection, French translation, spiritual guidance, Catholic Reformation, devotional literature</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the "To the Reader" preface from the previous page, further explaining the accessibility and universal appeal of Saint Teresa of Avila’s writing style in "The Way of Perfection."</meta>

# TO THE READER

The Saint <note>Saint Teresa of Avila</note> went to great lengths to humble her style as much as she could to make herself more intelligible <note>In the context of 16th-century spirituality, "lowering oneself" refers to adopting a simple, plain style of writing that avoids complex theological jargon to reach a wider audience.</note>. This is why it can be said of this Work that it is very useful, both for religious and cloistered individuals and for those who find themselves engaged in the affairs of the world. For the most spiritual and perfect souls will find here excellent rules to always advance further in the highest virtue; and those who, living in the secular world <note>The original French "dans le siècle" (in the century) refers to life in the physical world outside of a cloistered monastery.</note>, desire to serve God, will learn with what respect they must address Him in their prayers. They will also find here an admirable explanation of the Our Father <note>original: "Pater"</note>, which clearly demonstrates, through the excellent instructions it contains, the truth of what an ancient Church author said: that this Prayer of the Lord is the summary of the entire Gospel <note>This likely refers to Tertullian (c. 155 – c. 220 AD), who famously described the Lord's Prayer as "the epitome of the whole Gospel."</note>.

<summary>This preface concludes by praising Saint Teresa's accessible writing style and explaining that her work serves both monastics and laypeople, particularly through her insightful commentary on the Lord's Prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, The Way of Perfection, Lord's Prayer, Pater Noster, Christian humility, spiritual life, secular life vs. religious life, prayer instructions</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "To the Reader" preface, addressing the publishing history of this specific edition and offering final advice on how to approach Saint Teresa’s spiritual teachings.</meta>

# TO THE READER

Since it has been observed that the edition of this Book was entirely sold out <note>The original French "debitée" indicates that the previous printing had been fully distributed or purchased by the public.</note>, it has been deemed appropriate to reprint it exactly as it was given by its illustrious and pious Translator <note>This likely refers to Robert Arnauld d'Andilly (1589–1674), a prominent French writer whose translations of Saint Teresa were widely celebrated for their elegance and clarity.</note>, so that the Public may not be deprived of such an excellent guide for the spiritual life.

   It would be useless to further recommend this Treatise. All those who have any sense of piety will not be able to read it without esteeming it. And they will be truly happy if, in imitation of this admirable Saint <note>Saint Teresa of Avila</note> who drew these thoughts more from her heart than from her mind <note>original: "esprit." In this period, "heart" signifies the seat of divine love and sincere devotion, while "mind" or "spirit" refers to intellectual curiosity or academic study.</note>, they use them more to nourish their piety than to enlighten their soul; and if they take even more care to practice them than to read them.

<summary>This concluding note to the reader explains that the book was reprinted due to high demand and encourages the reader to focus on practicing the Saint's virtues rather than simply studying them intellectually.</summary>
<keywords>book publishing, piety, heart vs. mind, spiritual practice, Saint Teresa of Avila, Arnauld d'Andilly, religious devotion</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Following the "To the Reader" section from the previous page, this page begins the "Approbation," which is an official formal approval by theological authorities (Doctors of Theology) confirming that the work contains no errors in faith or morals.</meta>

<note>A decorative border consisting of a row of stylized floral ornaments marks the top of the page.</note>

# APPROBATION
# *of the Doctors.*

The favors that Saint Teresa received from Heaven are so extraordinary and so elevated that one cannot easily understand them without having had the experience and feeling of them in the heart; and whatever idea one might conceive of them, it would be difficult to find words in common language to express them well. To recognize their truth, it was necessary for them to be examined by Saints, who shared in the same graces and who had the gift of discerning spirits <note>This refers to the "discernment of spirits," a Christian practice of determining whether a person's spiritual impulses or visions come from God, the self, or a malevolent source.</note>; and it was necessary that this Saint take up the pen, and that she herself write her history <note>This refers to Saint Teresa’s autobiography, *The Life of Saint Teresa of Avila by Herself*.</note> to make them known to the whole Church. She could have no interpreter in our language who could better sustain, in a translation, the greatness of her thoughts and the force of her words than Monsieur d'Andilly <note>Robert Arnauld d'Andilly (1589–1674) was a prominent figure in the Jansenist movement and a celebrated translator of religious texts.</note>, who

<summary>This page presents an official theological approval (Approbation) of Saint Teresa's writings, arguing that her mystical experiences are so profound they require both holy witnesses and an eloquent translator like Arnauld d'Andilly to be understood.</summary>
<keywords>Approbation, Saint Teresa of Avila, mysticism, discernment of spirits, Arnauld d'Andilly, translation, divine grace, Catholic Church</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual endorsements (Approbations) seen on the previous page to the legal authorization, known as the "Privilege," which was required to print and sell books in 17th-century France.</meta>

[Horizontal line]

*Extract of the King’s Privilege.* <note>The "Privilege du Roy" was a legal monopoly granted by the French crown, giving a specific printer the exclusive right to publish a work for a set number of years. It functioned as an early form of copyright protection.</note>

**B** Y grace and Privilege of His Majesty granted at the King’s Council of State, held at Versailles on August 3, 1675, permission is granted to PIERRE LE PETIT, his Ordinary Printer, to print or reprint the works of Saint Teresa, in the translation of Mr. Arnauld d’Andilly, for a period and duration of fifty years, with prohibitions against all others from counterfeiting the said works, under the penalties set forth in the Original of the said Privilege.

The aforementioned Privilege was ceded and transferred to Mr. Denys Thierry, Printer and Bookseller in Paris, as appears by the official record of the sale of the assets <note>original: "vente du Fond." This refers to the sale of the printer's business inventory and legal rights.</note> of the said Mr. Le Petit by Ganneau, Sergeant-at-Mace <note>term: Sergent à Verge. An officer of the court responsible for executing legal orders; the "verge" or mace was his symbol of authority.</note> at the Châtelet of Paris <note>term: Châtelet. The judicial and police headquarters of Paris during the Old Regime.</note>, on April 21, 1687.

And Mr. Roulland acquired the aforementioned Privilege from Mr. Thierry, to enjoy it in his place and stead.

*FORE-* <note>This is a catchword, a common feature in old books where the first word of the following page is printed at the bottom of the current one to help the binder assemble the pages in the correct order. The next section likely begins with "Foreword" (Avant-propos).</note>

<summary>This page contains the "King’s Privilege," a legal document granting exclusive publishing rights for the works of Saint Teresa to a specific series of Parisian printers and booksellers between 1675 and 1687.</summary>
<keywords>King's Privilege, Pierre Le Petit, Denys Thierry, Roulland, copyright history, 17th-century printing, Versailles, Châtelet of Paris</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the legal "Privilege" and transfer of rights for the publication. This page begins the "Avant-propos" (Foreword) written by Saint Teresa of Avila herself, marking the transition from the book's legal front matter to her personal spiritual introduction.</meta>

### ->FOREWORD<-
#### ->of the Saint.<-

**T**he Sisters of this Monastery of Saint Joseph of Avila, knowing that Father <term>Presentatus</term> <note>original: "Pere Preſenté." This was a specific academic title within the Dominican Order for a friar who had completed his studies and been "presented" to the authorities to receive his Master of Theology degree.</note> Domingo Báñez, a religious of the Order of the glorious Saint Dominic, who is currently my Confessor, has permitted me to write about <term>Prayer</term> <note>original: "l'Oraiſon." In the 17th-century context, this specifically refers to *oraison mentale* or "mental prayer"—an internal, contemplative dialogue with God as opposed to the recitation of set vocal prayers.</note>, they believed that I could do so usefully, because I have discussed this subject with several very spiritual and very holy persons; and they have urged me so much to tell them something about it, that I have resolved to obey them, because the great love they bear for me will make them better receive what comes to them from me, however imperfect and poorly written it may be, than books whose style is excellent, and which have been made by men very learned in this matter. I put my confidence in their prayers, which might perhaps obtain from God that, by giving me the means to [tell] them

é <note>This letter "é" is a catchword, a common printing practice where the first syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom right to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>In this foreword, Saint Teresa explains that she is writing about mental prayer at the request of her fellow nuns in Avila and with the permission of her confessor, Domingo Báñez. She emphasizes that her sisters prefer her simple, heartfelt writing over more academic works because of their personal bond with her.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, Domingo Báñez, mental prayer, Dominican Order, Saint Joseph of Avila, religious obedience, spiritual direction</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Foreword" (Avant-propos) of Saint Teresa of Avila's "The Way of Perfection." The previous page ended with Teresa expressing hope that the sisters' prayers would obtain from God the means for her to provide useful instruction.</meta>

give, I will say something useful regarding the way of life practiced in this house. If I go astray, Father <term>Báñez</term> <note>original: "Pere Bagnez." Domingo Báñez (1528–1604) was a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and Teresa’s primary confessor and censor during the writing of this work.</note>, who will be the first to see it, will correct it or burn it. Thus, I shall lose nothing for having obeyed these servants of God; and they will know what I can do of my own accord when His grace does not assist me.

My design is to teach remedies for slight <term>temptations</term> <note>In the context of 16th-century monastic life, "temptations" often referred not just to major sins, but to distractions, spiritual dryness, or subtle movements of the ego that hindered "mental prayer."</note> stirred up by the devil, which religious persons <note>Teresa refers here to the nuns within her reformed Carmelite convents.</note> disregard because they do not believe them to be significant; and to also treat other points according as our Lord gives me the understanding, and as I am able to remember them. For, not knowing what I have to say, I would not know how to say it in order; and I believe it is best not to maintain any order at all, since it is already such a great reversal of order that I should even undertake to write on such a subject.

I implore the assistance of God, that I may conform myself entirely to His holy will. It is toward this that

<summary>Saint Teresa continues her foreword, explaining that her confessor, Father Báñez, will review her work for errors. She outlines her goal to address minor spiritual temptations and admits her writing lacks a formal structure, viewing her role as an author as a humble "reversal of order."</summary>

<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, Domingo Báñez, spiritual direction, temptations, monastic life, humility, mental prayer</keywords>

### *Of the Saint.*

...all my desires, even though my actions do not match them. But at least I do not lack affection and fervor to help, with all my power, my dear Sisters to advance further and further in the service of God. This love that I have for them, combined with my age and my experience of what occurs in some Religious Houses <note>In this period, "Religious Houses" refers to monastic communities such as convents or monasteries.</note>, will perhaps mean that in small matters I will find better success than the Scholars <note>original: "Sçavans"; the author refers to male theologians or academic authorities whose focus was often on complex dogma rather than the practical, daily struggles of cloistered women.</note>, because they, having other more important occupations and being strong people, do not take much account of these imperfections which seem to be nothing in themselves, and they do not consider that women being weak <note>The author uses the "weakness" of women—a common rhetorical device in 17th-century religious writing—to argue for the necessity of a specialized, gentler form of spiritual direction.</note>, everything is capable of harming them. 

Added also is the fact that the tricks which the devil employs against Nuns so strictly enclosed are great in number, because he knows that he needs new weapons to combat them. And since I have defended myself so poorly, being as wretched as I am, I would wish that my Sisters might profit from my mistakes.

<summary>The author explains her motivation for writing, suggesting that her lived experience in a convent allows her to understand the subtle spiritual challenges of nuns better than academic theologians. She aims to share the lessons learned from her own failings to help her "Sisters" avoid the unique temptations of a cloistered life.</summary>

<keywords>Religious life, spiritual direction, convent, feminine piety, humility, temptation, spiritual warfare</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory material from the previous section. The author is establishing her authority to speak based on personal experience rather than abstract theology, while explaining why she is writing this specific text separately from her autobiography.</meta>

### *The Saint’s Foreword.*

I shall say nothing that I have not recognized through experience, either in myself or in others. And although I was commanded a few days ago to write an account of my life <note>This likely refers to the author's formal autobiography, which many mystics were required to write for the Inquisition or their superiors to ensure their visions were not heretical.</note>, and I included there some advice regarding Prayer <term>Prayer</term> <note>original: "l'Oraison"; in this context, it refers specifically to "Mental Prayer," a form of internal, contemplative communication with God that goes beyond the recitation of standard prayers.</note>; nevertheless, because my Confessor <note>The priest responsible for the author's spiritual guidance and for censoring or approving her writings.</note> will perhaps not want you to see it right now, I will repeat some of it here, and add other things that seem to me necessary. May our Lord be pleased to assist me, as I have already prayed, and make everything I write succeed to His greater glory.

->THE<-

I desired, on the contrary, that if it were possible, nothing should be lacking of all necessary things, acting in this like a weak and imperfect person, although I was moved to this more by a good intention than by a desire for a softer or more relaxed life.

Having learned at that same time of the troubles in France, the havoc that the heretics <note>Teresa refers to the French Wars of Religion (1562–1598) and the spread of Protestantism (specifically Calvinism/Huguenots), which she viewed as a spiritual catastrophe for the Church.</note> were causing there, and how much this unfortunate sect was strengthening itself from day to day; I was so deeply moved by it that, as if I could do something or were something myself, I wept in the presence of God and prayed Him to remedy so great an evil. It seemed to me that I would have given a thousand lives to save a single one of that great number of souls who were being lost in that Kingdom. 

But seeing that I was only a woman <note>In the 16th century, women were barred from preaching or active ministry; Teresa's response was to create a "spiritual militia" through cloistered prayer.</note>, and moreover so wicked and quite incapable of rendering to my God the service I desired, I believed—as I believe still—that since He has so many enemies and so few f- <note>The text breaks off mid-word; the context of the work suggests the word is "friends" (amis).</note>

friends <note>Continuing the sentence from the previous page: "since He has so many enemies and so few friends."</note>, I ought to work with all my power to make these latter ones good.

Thus I resolved to do what depended on me to practice the **evangelical counsels** <note>The three traditional vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience, based on the teachings of Jesus in the Gospels.</note> with the greatest perfection I could, and to strive to lead this small number of **Religious women** <note>Nuns; specifically the first members of the Discalced Carmelite reform.</note> who are here to do the same thing. In this design I trusted in the great goodness of God, who never fails to assist those who renounce everything for the love of Him; I hoped that, these good daughters being such as my desire imagined them, my faults would be covered by their virtues, and I believed that we could please God in some measure by occupying ourselves entirely with praying for the **Preachers**, for the defenders of the Church, and for the **learned men** <note>Original: "hommes sçavans"; the theologians and scholars whose role was to argue against Protestant doctrines during the Counter-Reformation.</note> who maintain her cause. 

For in this way we would do what was in our power to help our Master, whom those traitors—who are indebted to Him for so many blessings—treat with such indignity that it seems they would wish to crucify Him again.

ON PERFECTION. Ch. I. 5

...tal? <note>original: "mortel." This completes the word from the previous page, describing the "mortal" or "deadly" atmosphere of the secular world.</note> For who can doubt that these persons <note>Teresa is referring to the Protestant reformers and those leaving the Catholic Church, whom she views as being led astray by the devil.</note> are already under the power of the devil? They are worthy of this punishment, since their works have deserved it; and it is only right that their delights and their false pleasures should have eternal fire as their reward. Let them then enjoy, since they so desire, this unhappy fruit of their actions. I confess, however, that I cannot see so many souls being lost without being overcome with grief. I know that for those who are already lost, there is no longer any remedy. But I wish that at least no more of them would perish.

O my Daughters in **JESUS CHRIST**, help me to pray to our Lord to deign to remedy such a great evil. It is for this reason that we are assembled here: this is the object of our <term>vocation</term> <note>From the Latin "vocare" (to call); it refers to the divine call to enter the religious life of the convent.</note>: it is the rightful subject of our tears: it is what we must occupy ourselves with: it is where all our desires must tend: it is what we must unceasingly ask of God, and we must not employ ourselves in what concerns secular affairs. ### CHAPTER II.

*That the Nuns should not trouble themselves with their temporal needs. Of the advantages found in poverty. Against large buildings.*

**D**O not imagine, my Sisters, that by failing to satisfy people of the world, you will lack the means to live. Never claim to sustain your House through human inventions and clever schemes <note>original: "inventions & des adresses humaines." Teresa warns against using social or political maneuvering to secure funding for the convent.</note>: otherwise you will die of hunger; and rightly so. Only cast your eyes upon your divine Spouse <note>A central metaphor in mystical theology, where the religious soul or the nun is viewed as the "Bride of Christ."</note>, since it is He who must nourish you.

12 [space] THE WAY

...poor in spirit <note>This completes the word "pauvre" (poor) from the previous page. "Poor in spirit" refers to the first Beatitude (Matthew 5:3), signifying a state of humble dependence on God rather than material or intellectual pride.</note>, as I had made a vow to be, I was for a long time foolish in spirit <note>original: "folle d'esprit." In this context, Teresa likely refers to her earlier years before her spiritual "conversion," during which she felt she lacked the true internal understanding of the poverty she had formally promised to uphold.</note>: and thus the more I was deprived of such a great good, the more I have recognized by experience that it is an extreme happiness for a soul to possess it.
   This happy poverty is such a great good that it encompasses all the goods of the world. Yes, I say it again, it encompasses all the goods of the world, since to despise the world is to be the master of the world. For what would I care about having the favor of the Great <note>In the 16th and 17th centuries, "the Great" (les Grands) referred to the high nobility and influential aristocrats.</note> and of Princes if I wanted neither to have their wealth, nor to enjoy their pleasures, and if I would be very distressed to do anything to please them that could displease God in the slightest thing? How could I also desire their vain honors, knowing that the greatest honor of a poor person consists in being truly poor? I hold that honors and riches almost always go hand in hand: He who loves honor cannot hate riches; and he who despises riches cares little for honor.

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that the vow of poverty brings a paradoxical "mastery" over the world, as a person who desires nothing from the powerful cannot be controlled by them. She concludes that the pursuit of social honor and the pursuit of wealth are inextricably linked.</summary>
<keywords>poverty of spirit, holy poverty, mastery of the world, detachment, favor of princes, honor and riches, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

### OF PERFECTION. Ch. II. 17

noise? <note>Continuing the thought from the previous page: if a grand building falls, it makes a spectacle, but the collapse of a humble house for the poor is hardly noticed.</note> Should the truly poor make any? And would anyone have compassion for them if they did?

TO return to the principal subject which has gathered us in this House <note>The "House" refers to the Convent of Saint Joseph in Ávila, the first monastery of the Discalced Carmelite reform, founded by Teresa in 1562.</note>, and for which I wish that we might do something agreeable to God, I say, that seeing that the heresy <note>Teresa is referring to the Protestant Reformation—specifically the religious wars in France—which she perceived as a "devouring fire" threatening the Catholic Church.</note> which has arisen in this century is like a devouring fire that always makes new progress, and that the power of men is not capable of stopping it, it seems to me that we must act as a Prince would, who, seeing that his enemies would ravage all his country, and that he would not be strong enough to resist them in the open field <note>Original: "en campagne." ### OF PERFECTION. Ch. III. 19

-ly fortify, from which he could make, with this small number, sorties against them that would trouble them much more than large, poorly trained troops could do. For it often happens that by this means one remains victorious; and at the very worst, one could only perish by famine, since there are no traitors among such people. Now here, my Sisters, famine may indeed press us; but it cannot force us to surrender. It may well make us die, but not defeat us.

Now, why do I tell you this? It is to make you understand that what we must ask of God is that He does not permit anyone in this place where good Christians have retreated to go and throw themselves onto the side of the enemy; but that He strengthens the virtue and courage of the Preachers and Theologians, who are like the leaders of these troops, and ensures that the Religious <note>In the 16th century, "Religious" specifically refers to those living under monastic vows, such as monks, nuns, and friars.</note> who compose the greatest number of His soldiers, advance day by day in the perfection that such a vocation requires.

*The Saint exhorts her Religious sisters to the observation of their Rule. That the Religious must love one another extremely. <meta>This page continues the opening of Chapter IV, where Saint Teresa transitions from the external purpose of the order (praying for the Church) to the internal spiritual requirements of the sisters themselves, emphasizing the strict observance of their Rule and Constitutions.</meta>

## ON PERFECTION. Ch. IV. 31

For what must we be like, in order not to be considered reckless in the judgment of God and of men? It is evident that we must work hard for this, and that to succeed, it is necessary to raise our thoughts very high, so that we may make such great efforts that our works correspond to them. For there is reason to hope that Our Lord will grant our prayers, provided that we forget nothing of what may depend on us to observe our Constitutions and our Rule exactly. I impose nothing new upon you, my Daughters. I ask you only to observe the things to which your vocation and your profession obligate you, although there is a great difference between the various ways in which one fulfills them.

The first Rule commands us to pray without ceasing: and as this precept contains the most important of our duties, if we observe it exactly, we will fail neither in the fasts, nor the disciplines <note>In this context, "disciplines" refers to formal acts of penance, often involving self-flagellation or other physical mortifications common in 16th-century monastic life.</note>, nor the silence to which our Institute <note>The term "Institute" here refers to the specific founding principles and established laws of the Discalced Carmelite Order.</note> obliges us; since you know that all—

36 ->THE WAY<-

As for this last kind of friendship, I would wish that in large monasteries many of them were found. For in this house, where we are and can only be thirteen <note>Teresa originally limited her reformed convents to 13 members to ensure a strictly communal, family-like environment where no one could form isolated sub-groups.</note>, all the **Sisters** must be friends; all must cherish one another; all must love one another; all must assist one another; and however holy they may be, I entreat them for the love of **Our Lord** to take great care against these *singularities* <note>original: *singularitez*. 38 [space] THE WAY

I have hardly ever fallen into them myself. I have often noticed them, and I greatly fear that they are found in most monasteries, as I have seen in several, because I know that nothing is more capable of preventing nuns from reaching great perfection, and that in Superiors <note>The leaders or "Mothers" of the religious houses.</note>, as I have already said, it is a plague <note>original: *peſte*. Teresa uses this strong language to describe a spiritual contagion that can infect and destroy the unity of a religious community.</note>.

Extreme care must be taken to cut the root of these partialities <note>original: *partialitez*. These are the cliques or "special" friendships that create divisions within a community meant to be united in God.</note> and these dangerous friendships as soon as they begin to arise. But it must be done with skill and with more love than rigor. An excellent remedy for this is to be together only at the appointed hours, and not to speak at all, just as we practice now; but to remain separated as the Rule <note>The *Rule of Saint Albert*, the governing document of the Carmelite Order, which emphasizes solitude and silence.</note> commands, and for each of us to withdraw to our own cell. Thus, although it is a laudable custom to have a common room where one works, I exhort you not to have one in this monastery, because it is much easier to maintain silence

->CHAPTER V<-

*Continuation of the same subject. How important it is that Confessors be learned. In what cases one may change. And concerning the authority of Superiors.*

<margin>On the need for learned Confessors.</margin>

I pray to God with all my heart that He never allows any of you to experience, in a Monastery of such strict enclosure, these troubles of spirit and these anxieties of which I have just spoken to you. For if the Prioress and the Confessor are on too good of terms <note>original: "sont bien ensemble." This implies a collusive or overly harmonious relationship where the Prioress and Confessor shield each other, leaving the nuns without an independent avenue for grievances or spiritual guidance.</note>, so that one dares say nothing to her of what concerns him, nor to him of what regards her, it is then that one will find oneself tempted to remain silent in confession regarding very important sins, out of fear of the trouble and anxiety that would result from speaking them. the devil can work by this means; and how dearly this dangerous restraint and this unfortunate point of honor <note>The "point of honor" (point d’honneur) was a pervasive social code in the 16th and 17th centuries, where the outward reputation of an institution was often prioritized over individual well-being.</note> cost! For by the false belief that the reputation of the Monastery depends on having only one Confessor, this infernal spirit puts these poor girls into a torment of spirit <note>original: "gesne d'esprit." Praise God exceedingly, my Daughters, for the liberty you now have to act in another way: for although it should not extend to having a great many Confessors, you may, in addition to the ordinary ones, have some who can clarify your doubts for you. ON PERFECTION. Ch. V. 55

<margin>On the authority of Superiors.</margin>

Among the inconveniences that can happen, as they are always encountered everywhere during this life, it seems to me that the least is that there be no Vicar <term>Vicar: An ecclesiastical official appointed by the Bishop to exercise authority or oversight over a religious community.</term> nor Confessor who has the power to enter, to command, and to depart, but only to watch over and take care that the House remains in recollection <note>Recollection refers to a state of quiet, inward prayer and focus on God, which was the cornerstone of Teresa’s Carmelite reform.</note>, that all things are done with propriety, and that progress is made both interiorly and exteriorly in the practice of virtue; so that if it is found that something is lacking, he may inform the Bishop; but he should not be the Superior. 

This is what is currently observed here, not by my advice alone, but by that of Monseigneur Don Alvaro de Mendoza <note>Don Alvaro de Mendoza (d. 1581) was the Bishop of Avila and a crucial ally who helped Teresa establish her first reformed convent, San José.</note>, now our Bishop and under whose guidance we remain—a person of very noble birth, a great servant of God, most affectionate toward everything concerning the religious life <note>original: "la Religion"</note> and all matters of piety, and who is moved by a very particular inclination to favor this House, which for several reasons is not yet submitted to...

E iiij <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of the book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa argues that the house's Vicar or Confessor should serve as an overseer rather than an absolute superior to prevent abuses of power. she credits Bishop Alvaro de Mendoza for supporting this balanced governance structure.</summary>
<keywords>ecclesiastical authority, Bishop Alvaro de Mendoza, monastic discipline, recollection, convent governance, Carmelite reform</keywords>

56 THE WAY

the Order <note>This completes the thought from the previous page: the convent of San José was initially placed under the jurisdiction of the Bishop rather than the superiors of the Carmelite Order.</note>. Having gathered learned, spiritual men of great experience to consider this matter, they reached the conclusion I have just described. This decision followed many prayers from various people, to which I joined my own, despite my wretchedness <note>Teresa frequently uses self-deprecating language, such as "miserable" or "wretched," which was a common rhetorical device of humility in 16th-century spiritual writing.</note>. Thus, it is only right that in the future, the Mother Superiors <term>Superiors: The heads of individual convents, responsible for the spiritual and administrative life of the sisters.</term> follow this counsel. It is the path upon which so many good people have agreed after asking God for the light necessary to recognize the best course of action—and it is undoubtedly the best, as results have shown thus far. I pray that He may grant that this arrangement continues forever, provided it be for His glory. Amen <note>original: "Ainsi soit-il"</note>.

<summary>Saint Teresa concludes her argument for the convent's specific governance structure, noting it was established through the consensus of spiritual experts and divine guidance. She urges future Superiors to maintain this proven path for the glory of God.</summary>

**CHAPTER VI.**

*On the spiritual love that one must have*
*for God, and for those who can*
*contribute to our salvation.*

[Large initial Q] <note>The initial letter "Q" begins the word "Quoique" (Although).</note> Although I have made a long digression, what I have said is so important <margin>Concerning the love of God, which is entirely spiritual.</margin> that I am sure those who truly understand its significance will not blame me for it.

I now return to that love which we are not only permitted to have, but which it is beneficial for us to possess. I say that it is purely spiritual; and in naming it so, I do not know if I quite know what I am saying. <note>Teresa often uses this self-deprecating style to express the difficulty of describing mystical or internal spiritual states in human language.</note> It seems to me that it is not necessary to speak of it at length, for I fear that few among you possess it; and if there is anyone whom Our Lord favors with such a grace, she must praise Him greatly for it, because such a great gift will undoubtedly be accompanied by a very high state of perfection. <term>Perfection: In this context, "perfection" refers to the "Way of Perfection"—a state of spiritual maturity and union with God's will, rather than the absence of all human error.</term> Nevertheless, I wish to [tell] you...

You will perhaps tell me that these persons do not know what it is to love, or how to respond to the friendship shown to them. I answer that at least they care little for being loved; and although at first nature may sometimes make them rejoice to see that they are loved, no sooner do they return to themselves than they realize it is but a folly—except regarding those who can contribute to their salvation through their prayers or through their doctrine. ### ON PERFECTION. Ch. VI. 61

weary <note>original: "ennuyent"; the text completes the word started on the previous page.</note> them, because they know that these affections can be of no benefit to them, and might even be capable of causing them harm. Nevertheless, they do not fail to be grateful for it, and they repay this love by commending to God those who love them. For they consider the affection of these people as a debt with which Our Lord is charged: because, seeing nothing in themselves that deserves to be loved, they believe that they are only loved because God loves them. Thus, they leave to Him the care of repaying the love that people have for them, and by praying to Him with all their heart, they believe themselves to be discharged of it, and remain as tranquil as if this affection did not affect them at all.

These considerations make me think sometimes that there is a great deal of blindness in this desire to be loved, unless it be, as I have said, by those who can help us to acquire eternal riches <note>original: "biens éternels"; refers to spiritual merits or the rewards of heaven.</note>. If it seems to you that these persons therefore love nothing but God, I answer you that they also love their neighbor, and with a love more true, more useful, and even greater than others do, because they always much prefer—even in regard to God—to give than to receive. This would be impossible for them if they did not possess virtue and did not love God greatly.

I say impossible: for even though these persons might have an ardent love for them; though they might overwhelm them with benefits; though they might render them every imaginable service, and though they might even be adorned with all the graces of nature; these holy souls could not bring themselves, by these considerations alone, to love them with a firm and lasting love. ->OF PERFECTION. Ch. VII. 69<-

...least pain without us almost losing patience; and so it is with everything else. But it is not the same with this other love, which is entirely of charity. For even though our infirmity <note>Our human weakness or natural emotional state.</note> makes us sensitive to the troubles of the person we love, our reason comes immediately to our aid and makes us consider if these troubles are useful for their salvation, if they strengthen them in virtue, and in what manner they bear them. One then prays to God to give them the patience they need, so that their sufferings may earn them merit <note>In this context, "merit" refers to the spiritual growth or divine favor gained by enduring trials with faith.</note> and profit them.

If one sees that He grants this patience, the distress one felt is changed into consolation and joy—even though the affection one bears for them means one would rather suffer than see them suffer, if by suffering in their place one could acquire for them the merit found in suffering. But this happens without feeling either turmoil or anxiety.

perfect love bore for us, since they would want to be able to take all these pains upon themselves, so that these persons might profit from them without suffering. Whatever the Superior <note>The "Superior" refers to the Mother Superior or Prioress of the convent, who holds authority over the community.</note> commands you <margin>Various excellent pieces of advice.</margin> to do may seem harsh to you, do not show any sign of it, unless it be to her alone, and with humility; since if you acted otherwise, you would do great harm to all your Sisters.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is important to know which things one should feel, and in what way one should have compassion for one's Sisters. One must always be deeply moved by the slightest faults one sees them commit, if they are manifest <note>original: "manifestes." In this context, it means clearly visible or public faults rather than private ones.</note>; and one could not better show the love one bears them than by bearing with them and not being surprised by them: which will result in them also bearing with yours, which, although you may not notice them, are undoubtedly greater in number. You must also strongly recommend these persons to God, and strive to practice with great perfection the virtues contrary to the faults you notice in them, because you must much rather strive to instruct them by your actions than by your words. In this period, a woman usually provided a dowry (money or property) to the convent upon entry. If she were sent away, the convent was often required to return these funds so she could be supported elsewhere.</note>. But if all that I have just said is useless, she must be shut in a prison <note>Historically, monasteries maintained rooms for "monastic imprisonment"—a form of strict solitary confinement used as a disciplinary measure to prevent a person's behavior from corrupting the rest of the community.</note> from which she never leaves, since it is much better to treat her with this just severity than to allow her to poison all the others. ### CHAPTER VIII.

*That it is of the highest importance to detach oneself*
*from everything in order to attach oneself only to*
*God. On the extreme happiness of the*
*Religious vocation. The Saint’s humility*
*on this subject. That a Nun must*
*not be attached to*
*her relatives.*

<margin>On the need to attach oneself only to God.</margin> **I** come now to the detachment <note>In Carmelite spirituality, "detachment" (*desasimiento*) refers to the voluntary release of emotional or material ties to anything other than God, including family, possessions, and even one’s own will.</note> in which we must remain, and which is of the highest importance if it is perfect. Yes, I say it again, it is of the highest importance if it is perfect. For when we attach ourselves only to our sole Creator, and regard all created things as nothing <note>The concept of "nothingness" (*nada*) is central to the writings of Saint Teresa and Saint John of the Cross, suggesting that compared to the infinite nature of God, the material world has no independent substance.</note>, His sovereign Majesty fills our soul with so many virtues, that provided we advance little by little by working with all our might, we will not afterwards have much to fight; because Our Lord will arm Himself for our defense against demons and against the world.

<summary>Saint Teresa introduces the concept of detachment, arguing that when a soul turns entirely toward God and views the world as "nothing," God provides the virtues and protection necessary to overcome spiritual obstacles.</summary>
<keywords>detachment, Saint Teresa of Avila, spiritual warfare, virtues, religious life, Carmelite spirituality, sovereignty of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter VIII of "The Way of Perfection." On the previous page, Saint Teresa introduced the necessity of detaching oneself from created things to be filled by God. Here, she expands on the gratitude for the religious life and her own feelings of inadequacy in practicing the virtues she describes.</meta>

34          THE WAY

Do you believe, my daughters <note>Teresa of Avila addresses the nuns of her convent as "daughters" or "sisters," reflecting her role as Mother Foundress and Prioress.</note>, that it is a small matter to procure for ourselves such a great good as that of giving ourselves entirely to God, without division or sharing; since all good things are in Him as in their source? Let us give Him a thousand thanks, my sisters, that it has pleased Him to gather us and unite us in a place where we speak of nothing else. But why tell you this, since there is not one among you who is not capable of instructing me, and that while it is so important to be detached from everything, I see myself so far from being so as much as I would wish, and as I understand one ought to be?

I could say the same of all the virtues of which I speak in this discourse, since it is more difficult to practice them than to write about them, and I even perform poorly in this latter task, because sometimes only experience <note>Teresa frequently emphasizes that spiritual wisdom is gained through lived "experience" (*experiencia*) and the "way of prayer" rather than through formal theological education.</note> can allow one to speak well of them. Thus, if it happens that I am not mistaken in something, it is because opposites are recognized through their opposites <note>This is a reference to the philosophical principle that one understands a quality better when contrasted with its opposite—for instance, understanding the peace of detachment by the turmoil of being attached to worldly things.</note>, and I have learned to know these

<summary>Saint Teresa expresses gratitude for the monastic community's focus on God and humbles herself by admitting that writing about detachment is far easier than practicing it perfectly.</summary>

### OF PERFECTION. Ch. VIII. 85

virtues by falling into the vices which are contrary to them.

<margin>On the happiness of the Religious vocation</margin> As for the external, one sees well enough how much we are separated from all things in this retreat <note>Teresa refers to the "enclosure" of the Discalced Carmelite reform, where nuns lived in strict separation from the world to focus entirely on prayer.</note>; and it seems that Our Lord, in bringing us here, wished to separate us from everything in this manner, to remove the obstacles that might prevent us from drawing near to Him.

O my Lord and my master, how could I for my part, and how could we all have deserved such a great favor as that which you have done us, by deigning to seek us out and choose us from among so many others to communicate yourself so particularly to us? May it please your divine goodness that we do not make ourselves unworthy, through our own fault, of such a grace. I entreat you, my Daughters, in the name of Almighty God, to reflect on the extreme obligation we have toward Him for having brought us to this House <note>This "House" is St. Joseph’s in Avila, the first convent of Teresa's reform, which was noted for its poverty and small number of inhabitants.</note>. Let each of you enter into herself to consider this well, and place before her eyes that of only twelve—

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter VIII of "The Way of Perfection," following Saint Teresa's exhortation for her sisters to reflect on the rarity of their selection. She transitions from the general grace of a religious vocation to her personal experience of needing a smaller, more disciplined environment to correct her faults.</meta>

...having been called to Religious life is so great. But since I have responded very poorly to such a holy vocation, You did not wish, Lord, to leave me any longer on my own reputation <note>original: "sur ma foy," literally "on my faith." In this context, it suggests being taken at her word or relied upon based on her outward status as a nun.</note> in a monastery <note>This refers to the Convent of the Incarnation in Avila, where Teresa lived for decades before her reform. It was a large, less strictly enclosed community where she felt her spiritual laxity was easily overlooked.</note> where, among the great number of Nuns who were there, so many were virtuous that the disorder of my life <note>Teresa often speaks with great humility about her "disordered" life, referring to a period of years where she felt her prayer was lukewarm and her heart was divided between God and worldly friendships.</note> could not have been known—which I would have hidden myself, as I did for so many years. Thus You have brought me, my God, into this House <note>The "House" of St. Joseph’s in Avila, the first convent of the Discalced Carmelite reform, which was much smaller and more austere.</note>, where, there being only such a small number of persons, it is almost impossible that...

In the 17th century, this term encompassed all family and kin.</note> until she feels delivered from the desire to see them, and until she has obtained this grace from God after having prayed much for it. ## ON PERFECTION. Ch. IX. 89
[Horizontal line]
### CHAPTER IX.

How useful it is to detach oneself from excessive affection for one’s relatives. And that one receives more assistance from the friends God gives than from one’s kin <term>kin: translated from "parens," which in the 17th century referred to all family members and relations, not just parents</term>.

<margin>On detachment from relatives</margin>
I F we who are Nuns <term>Nuns: "Religieuses," women living under religious vows in a community</term> knew the harm we suffer by conversing much with our relatives, how would we not flee from them? I confess that I do not understand—even setting aside what concerns God—what advantage we can receive from them for our consolation and our peace. Since we are neither able nor permitted to take part in their pleasures, we can only feel their sorrows and shed tears over their troubles, sometimes more than they themselves shed. Thus, I can boldly say to these Nuns that if they receive some satisfaction for their senses <note>The "senses" refers to worldly, natural, or emotional gratification as opposed to spiritual joy.</note> from them,

### CHAPTER X.

*That it is not enough to detach oneself from*
*one's relatives if one does not detach*

<margin>On detachment from oneself.</margin>
WHEN we are thus detached from the world and from our relatives, and we live enclosed within a monastery in the manner we have described, it may perhaps seem that everything is accomplished and that there are no more enemies left for us to fight. O my Sisters, do not hold this opinion, and take great care not to let yourselves fall asleep. You would be like someone who goes to bed without fear after having securely bolted their door for fear of thieves, but who already has them inside the house. There are no thieves more dangerous than those of the household; and as <note>Teresa uses the metaphor of the "domestic thief" to describe the human ego and self-will, which remain with the nun even after she has left the outside world behind.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa warns that physical enclosure in a monastery is not the end of the spiritual struggle, as the most dangerous enemies—self-will and the ego—reside within.</summary>

we ourselves are these interior and secret thieves; and because we always remain with ourselves, if we do not take very special care to constantly combat our own will, many things will be capable of making us lose this holy liberty of spirit. His only fear is to displease his God: and he prays to him constantly to strengthen him in these two virtues <note>Referring back to the "two sisters" mentioned on page 97: humility and self-mortification.</note>, so that he may not lose them through his own fault. It is a specific quality of these virtues to hide themselves from the one they enrich in such a way that he does not perceive them at all, nor can he believe he possesses them, no matter what one might say to persuade him. Now, although those who possess these virtues do not wish to be esteemed for what they truly are, they make themselves known despite their intentions, and one cannot interact with them without immediately perceiving their character.

## CHAPTER XI.

*Not to complain of slight indispositions. To suffer great ills with patience. Not to fear death: and what a happiness it is to subject the body to the spirit.*

<margin>Not to complain of slight indispositions.</margin>
It seems to me, my Sisters, that it is a very great imperfection to complain constantly about small ailments. If you can suffer them, suffer them. If they are great, they will complain enough of themselves through another manner of complaint <note>Saint Teresa suggests that truly serious illnesses manifest themselves through visible symptoms or the sheer physical inability to function, making verbal complaints unnecessary.</note>, and will not be able to be hidden for long. Consider that being here in small number <note>The Discalced Carmelite convents founded by Teresa were intentionally kept small—usually limited to 13 or 21 sisters—to foster a close-knit, familial atmosphere and avoid the distractions of larger institutions.</note>, if you have charity, and one of you takes up this bad habit, she will give much trouble to all the others. As for those who are truly sick, they must say so and allow themselves to be assisted with what is necessary for them. For if you are once delivered from self-love <note><term>Self-love</term> (amour propre): In 17th-century spiritual writing, this refers to an ego-driven preoccupation with one's own comfort, reputation, or desires, which is seen as the primary obstacle to union with God.</note>, you will [remain...] <note>The word "ref-" at the end of the page likely completes to "restera" or "refroidiriez," but will be confirmed on the following page.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa counsels her sisters to practice restraint regarding minor physical complaints to avoid burdening the small community. She emphasizes that genuine illness will naturally become apparent and should be treated with proper care once self-centeredness is overcome.</summary>
<keywords>asceticism, community life, health, patience, self-love, Discalced Carmelites</keywords>

THE WAY OF PERFECTION. Ch. XI. 105

...feel the effects [of that deliverance] in such a way, even regarding the slightest of the kind treatments shown to you, that there will be no need to fear you might accept any without necessity, nor complain without cause. 

But when you have a legitimate reason, it will be as appropriate to speak of it as it would be wrong to seek relief without need. It would even be a great wrong if one failed to assist you with care at such a time. And you cannot doubt that this will be done in a house of prayer and charity, such as this one, where the number of people living here is so small that it is easy to notice one another’s needs. 

Break the habit, then, of complaining about certain weaknesses and ailments common to women <note>Teresa often speaks bluntly to her nuns about "feminine" complaints, which she believed could sometimes be amplified by the "imagination" or a lack of mental discipline.</note> which are not long-lasting, and with which the devil sometimes fills the imagination. Content yourselves, then, with speaking of them only to God. Otherwise, you run the risk of never being delivered from them.

I insist much upon this point because I consider it very important, and believe it is one of the things that cause—

Consider, I pray you, how many poor sick people there are who do not even have anyone to whom they can complain, since these two things do not go together: being poor and being well-treated. Reflect also on how many married women there are (for I know there are many, even of high status) who, although they suffer great pains, dare not complain of them for fear of upsetting their husbands. Alas! Sinner that I am; have we then come into the Religious Life <note>In this context, "Religion" refers to the state of being a nun or monk under Vows.</note> to be more at our ease than they are? to remain in enclosure, <note>Religious "enclosure" refers to the rule preventing nuns from leaving the monastery grounds, ensuring a life of contemplation separated from the world.</note> to attend the Choir and the Office, <note>The "Choir" refers to the communal singing of the "Divine Office," the set of prayers and psalms recited by religious orders at specific hours of the day and night.</note> and so many other labors, without being able to satisfy any desire we might have to relieve ourselves of them except very rarely—having perhaps been the only one to whom this [exception] has happened in so many Monasteries where I have been: why shall we not work to also mortify our interior? For if the interior is well-ordered, the exterior will be also; then there will be nothing we do not perform, not only with more perfection and merit, but with great sweetness and rest.

This is acquired little by little, as I have said, by resisting our own will even in the smallest things, until our body is entirely subjected to our spirit. I say it again: everything, or nearly everything, consists in renouncing the care of ourselves and that which concerns our own satisfaction. ### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XII. 113

...last hour, who would be the person who would not wish to employ it well?

Believe me, my Sisters, the safest course is to always keep these thoughts before our eyes. Let us learn, then, to oppose our own will in all things. For even if you do not succeed at it immediately, if you work toward it with care and through the means of Prayer, <note>The original French uses "Oraiſon," referring to mental prayer or the "Prayer of Quiet," a cornerstone of St. Teresa’s spiritual method.</note> you will arrive imperceptibly and without even realizing it at the height of this virtue. It is true that it seems quite harsh to say that we must never do our own will in anything; but that is only because one does not say, at the same time, how many pleasures and consolations accompany this mortification, <note>Mortification refers to the practice of self-denial or "dying" to one's own selfish desires to grow spiritually.</note> and the advantages one gains from it even during this life. Since you all practice this already, do I not have reason to say that the most difficult part is already done? You encourage one another; you help one another; and each of you strives in this to surpass her companion.

One must take extreme care <margin>Against the desire for precedence</margin> to restrain our internal impulses, especially regarding...

### OF PERFECTION. Ch. XII. 115

...at all, and they will think that what I say is too severe, since God does not cease to bestow His favors upon those who are not in such perfect detachment <note>Detachment (original: "détachement") refers to the spiritual practice of releasing one's emotional or ego-driven dependence on worldly things, status, or even spiritual consolations.</note>. I believe that when this happens, it is because He knows through His infinite wisdom that these souls need these favors in order to be able to resolve to abandon all things for the love of Him.

But I do not call entering into Religion <note>In this context, "Religion" refers specifically to the "religious life"—the formal state of being a monk or nun bound by vows.</note> "abandoning all things," since one can still find attachments and ties within Religion itself; and, on the contrary, there is no place where a perfect soul cannot be in detachment and humility. ### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XII. 117

...a soul that possesses humility, it is impossible that it should not be further strengthened in this virtue by reflecting seriously upon its entire life. For then it will see how little service it has rendered to God, and the extreme obligations it owes to Him; that marvelous lowering <note>original: "abaissement"; referring to the Divine Condescension or the Incarnation, where God takes on a lowly human form to reach humanity.</note> which made Him descend even to the soul to provide an example of humility; the multitude of its sins; and the place where those sins had made it deserve to be cast down <note>A reference to Hell.</note>. This will bring the soul a sense of confusion <note>In this spiritual context, "confusion" refers to a holy shame or a profound sense of one's own unworthiness before God.</note> which will be so advantageous to it, that this enemy of our salvation will not have—as I have said—the boldness to begin tempting it again, knowing well that all his efforts would be equally shameful and useless to him.

I have a piece of advice to give you regarding this, which I pray you to engrave forever in your memory. It is that if you desire to take revenge on the demon and to be soon delivered from these kinds of temptations, you must not only draw advantage from them in your interior <note>One's "interior" refers to the inner life of the soul and the private thoughts of the heart.</note>, since it would be a great imperfection to fail to do so;

### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XIV. 133

...ones. But when a person of good sense <note>original: *bon sens*. Teresa values practical judgment and natural reason as a necessary foundation for the supernatural gifts of prayer.</note> begins to grow fond of the good, she attaches herself to it strongly, because she knows that it is the best and the surest path; and even if she does not advance much in virtue, she will be able to serve others in many things, particularly by her good advice, without giving trouble to anyone. Instead, when intelligence <note>original: *l'esprit*. In this 17th-century context, this refers to mental clarity, wit, or the ability to discern and understand.</note> is lacking, I do not see in what way she could be useful to a community; but I see clearly that she could be very harmful to it.

This lack of intelligence cannot be recognized so quickly, because there are many who speak well, yet poorly understand what has been said to them; and others who, even though they speak little and rather poorly, reason well in many things. There are others who, being in a holy simplicity <note>A traditional spiritual concept referring to a soul that is unconcerned with worldly complexities and focused entirely on God.</note>, are very ignorant regarding the affairs and the way of acting in the world, yet are very learned in what must be conducted with God. This is why it is necessary to observe them closely before receiving them, and extreme—

<summary>St. Teresa argues that while worldly ignorance is acceptable, a lack of natural intelligence is harmful to a religious community. 134 ->THE WAY<-
tremely test them before allowing them to make their Profession <note>A formal ceremony where a nun takes her permanent vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience, fully entering the religious order.</note>. Let the world know once and for all that you have the liberty to send them away, because in a monastery where there are as many austerities <note>Severe self-discipline and avoidance of all forms of indulgence; the Discalced Carmelites practiced strict silence, fasting, and manual labor.</note> as in this one, you may have several reasons that oblige you to do so. And when it is known that we usually act in this manner, it will no longer be considered an insult.

I say this because the age in which we live is so unhappy, and our weakness so great, that even though our holy predecessors expressly recommended us to have no regard for what the world considers a dishonor, nevertheless, the fear of upsetting parents, and in order to avoid some insignificant gossip that would arise in the world, we fail to practice this ancient and so laudable custom. May God grant that those who receive them in this way <note>That is, receiving candidates out of social obligation rather than their fitness for the rule.</note> are not punished for it in the next life; although they never lack pretexts to make it believe that this can be done legitimately.

<summary>St. Teresa emphasizes the right of the monastery to dismiss unsuitable novices, arguing that maintaining the community's spiritual health is more important than avoiding social scandal or offending a candidate's family.</summary>
<keywords>Profession, austerities, monastery discipline, social reputation, discernment of spirits, Discalced Carmelites</keywords>

ON PERFECTION. Ch. XIV. 135

This is so important for all of you that each must consider it individually, recommend it strongly to Our Lord, and encourage the Prioress <note>French: *Superieure*. The head of the convent; St. Teresa uses this title interchangeably with Prioress.</note> to take careful watch over it. I pray to God with all my heart that he gives you the light that is necessary for this subject. I am convinced that when the Prioress examines, without self-interest and without passion, what is most useful for the good of the Monastery, God never allows her to be mistaken; and that, on the contrary, she cannot fail to go astray if she lets herself be swayed by these false compassions and these impertinent maxims of a prudence that is entirely worldly <note>French: *seculiere*; referring to the "secular" world outside the convent walls, which values social status and family connections over spiritual rigor.</note> and entirely human.

For it is undoubtedly an action of very great humility, and an imitation of Our Lord, to see oneself condemned without being in the wrong and to remain silent. I pray you then with all my heart to apply yourselves to this with care, since you can draw a great advantage from it; and on the contrary, I see no benefit in your excusing yourselves, except, as I have said, in certain occasions which could cause trouble <note>French: *peine*; can mean pain, difficulty, or trouble for others.</note> if one did not speak the truth.

138      THE WAY

tice of small things, one makes oneself capable of achieving victory in great things. <note>The text continues from the previous page: "...one can in the practice of small things..." St. Teresa uses a military metaphor common in her writing; by mastering small daily irritations or slights, the nun prepares her soul for the "great victory" of total spiritual detachment.</note>

    But how easy that is to say, and how poorly I practice it! It is true that I have never been able to experience it in matters of consequence <note>original: *choſes de conſequence*; meaning serious or significant trials.</note>, since I have never heard evil said of me without clearly seeing that there was reason to say much more; because even though what was said was not exactly as they said it, I had offended God in many other ways, and thus they were sparing me by not mentioning those instead. Moreover, I am always more pleased that I am blamed for what I have not done, rather than for what I have actually done.

    It helps greatly in acquiring this virtue to consider that one can lose nothing, and that one gains in various ways by practicing it, the principal of which is that it makes us imitate Our Lord in some way. I say "in some way," because all things considered, we are never accused of having failed without our having fallen into some fault, since <note>The page ends mid-sentence. St. Teresa is arguing that unlike Jesus, who was truly innocent, humans are always "guilty" of something in the eyes of God, making any earthly accusation somewhat deserved in a general sense.</note>

## CHAPTER XVII.

### ->That not all souls are suited for contemplation. That some arrive there late, and that others can only pray vocally. But that those who are truly humble must be content to walk in the path by which it pleases God to lead them.<-

<margin>On contemplation.</margin>

It seems that I am already entering into the subject of <term>Prayer</term> <note>original: "l'Oraiſon." In the 17th-century French translation of Teresa's work, "Oraison" specifically refers to "Mental Prayer"—the internal, heart-to-heart conversation with God—rather than just the recitation of set texts.</note>. But I have beforehand an important thing to say touching upon the humility so necessary in <term>this House</term> <note>St. Teresa is referring to the Monastery of St. Joseph in Avila, the first house of her Discalced Carmelite reform, where her sisters lived.</note>, since one must exercise oneself particularly in prayer there, and since humility is one of its principal parts. Now, how will he who is truly humble ever be able to imagine himself being as good as those who arrive as far as being contemplatives? God can nevertheless make it so, by His grace, that he be of this number. But if he believes me, he will place himself...

<summary>St. Teresa begins Chapter XVII by addressing the diversity of spiritual paths, noting that while not all are called to contemplation, humility is the essential foundation for whatever way God leads a soul.</summary>

# ON PERFECTION. Ch. XVII. 157

...always in the lowest place, as Our Lord has ordered us and taught us by His example. Let the soul dispose itself, then, to walk in the way of contemplation, if it is the will of God that it enter there. And if it is not, let humility lead her to consider herself happy to serve the servants of the Lord, and to bless <term>His Majesty</term> <note>original: "ſa Majeſté." St. Teresa frequently uses this royal title to refer to God, reflecting the courtly language of the Spanish Golden Age.</note> that He has deigned to let her enter into their holy company—she who deserved to be the companion and slave of demons.

I do not say this without great reason, since it is so important to know that God does not lead all persons in the same manner, and that he who appears the most debased in the eyes of men is perhaps the most elevated before His eyes. Thus, although the <term>Religious</term> <note>original: "Religieuſes." In this context, it refers specifically to the cloistered nuns of the Carmelite order.</note> of this Monastery all exercise themselves in <term>Prayer</term> <note>original: "l'Oraiſon." As previously noted, this refers to mental prayer—the internal practice of meditation—rather than vocal recitation.</note>, it does not follow that they are all contemplatives. That is impossible; it must be a great consolation for those who have not received this gift to know that it comes purely from God. Since it is a thing that is not...

<summary>St. Teresa explains that contemplation is a gift granted solely by God's will and that those not chosen for it should find peace and joy in humble service. She emphasizes that spiritual standing in God's eyes often differs from outward appearances of status.</summary>

Was Martha not a Saint, although it is never said that she was a contemplative? And what more could you wish for than to be able to resemble this blessed woman who merited receiving our Lord JESUS CHRIST in her house so many times, providing Him with food, serving Him, and sitting at His table? 

For if she had always been, like her sister, in <term>ecstasies</term> <note>original: "transports." praises the contemplatives with His own mouth and holds them as His friends, He also makes them suffer more than others. Thus, as He leads contemplatives by a path so harsh and rugged that they sometimes believe themselves to be lost and obliged to begin again, they have need to receive some refreshment from His goodness to sustain them. Now, this refreshment must not be only water, but a strong and powerful wine, so that by being divinely intoxicated <note>This "spiritual intoxication" is a common mystical metaphor for being so filled with God’s love that one becomes indifferent to worldly pain or physical hardship.</note> they may suffer courageously, and without even thinking about what they suffer.

     Thus I see few true contemplatives who are not very courageous and very resolute in suffering; because the first thing that Our Lord does in them when He sees them weak is to give them courage, and to remove from them the apprehension of labors. I imagine that however little those who are in the <term>active life</term> <note>The "active life" refers to the path of serving God through external works and duties, contrasted here with the "contemplative life" of interior prayer.</note> see them fa-

<summary>Teresa explains that God provides contemplatives with spiritual "refreshment" or "wine" to help them endure the great sufferings and trials inherent to their path. This divine strength removes their fear of hardship and grants them the courage necessary for their vocation.</summary>

      Among the properties of water, I remember that it has three which are relevant to my subject. The first is to refresh, for there is no heat so great that water cannot dampen it; and it even extinguishes the largest fires, unless they are "fireworks" <note>original: "feux d'artifice." In the 17th century, this term referred to chemical or "Greek fire" used in warfare, which burned even more fiercely when water was applied to it. St. Teresa uses this as a metaphor for divine love.</note>, which, on the contrary, it only increases. Oh, what a wonder, my God, to see that a fire not subject to the ordinary laws of nature has such a prodigious force that its opposite, in trying to extinguish it, only increases it further! I would have great need here of knowing Philosophy <note>St. Teresa often expresses her lack of formal education in "Natural Philosophy"—the 17th-century term for science—though she displays a keen intuitive understanding of physical properties.</note> to be able to explain myself well through the knowledge it would give me of the properties of things, and I would take great pleasure in it; but I do not know how to say it, and perhaps I do not even know what it is I want to say.

But even if a thousand temptations should come in a crowd, like a great sea, to extinguish the fire of which I speak, not only would it diminish none of its heat, but it would dissipate them all and remain fully victorious. If it is a water that falls from Heaven, instead of harming the fire, it only serves to redouble its ardor <note>term: <term>ardor</term> (ardeur). Its nature is to always ignite this divine fire more and more, and to spread it, if it were possible, throughout the whole world.

184      ->THE WAY<-

do you know well that this living water, this celestial water, this clear water of which I speak, cleanses souls in such a way that when—without being troubled or mixed with any mire <note>original: "fange." Teresa uses the metaphor of mud or filth to describe worldly attachments or the distractions of the human ego.</note>—it falls completely pure from Heaven, I hold for certain that a soul could not drink of it a single time without being purified of all its stains. For as I have said elsewhere, this water—which is nothing other than our union with God—being entirely supernatural and not depending at all on us, He only allows certain souls to drink of it to purify them of the stains of their sins, and to free them from the miseries that were an unhappy consequence of them.

As for those other sweetnesses <note>original: "douceurs." It sometimes passes into such an excess that it goes almost as far as to trouble the judgment, just as I saw happen not long ago to a person who—although intense <note>original: "violente." In this context, it refers to a passionate or fiery natural temperament rather than physical aggression.</note> by nature—is so accustomed to renouncing her own will (as she demonstrates on other occasions) that it seems she no longer has one. One would have believed that during that moment she had lost her mind, so excessive was the pain she suffered, and so great was the effort she made to hide it.

Upon which, I believe that in these encounters which are so extraordinary—even though this proceeds from the Spirit of God—it is a very praiseworthy humility to feel fear, because we must not persuade ourselves that we have such a great love for Him that it would be capable of reducing us to such a state. I say then...

<meta>This page continues Chapter 19 of "The Way of Perfection." 192 THE WAY

Even so, I would consider it useful, if this person is able (for perhaps she will not always be able to do so), that she should renounce this desire she has to die. She should do this by considering how little service she has rendered to God until now; that she might please Him more by preserving her life than by losing it; and that perhaps He wishes to use her to open the eyes of some soul who was on the verge of being lost. For by making herself more pleasing to His divine Majesty in this way, she will have reason to hope to possess Him one day more fully than she would have, had she died at that very moment.

This remedy <note>original: "remede." Teresa often uses medicinal metaphors for spiritual practices.</note> seems effective to me for softening such a pressing <term>sorrow: referring back to the "pleasant pain" of spiritual longing mentioned on the previous page</term>, and one will undoubtedly derive a great advantage from it. Since to serve God faithfully one must carry one's <term>cross: a common Christian metaphor for enduring earthly suffering or burdens in imitation of Jesus</term> here below, it is as if, to console a deeply afflicted person, one said to her: Have patience; surrender yourself to the guidance of God; pray to Him to accomplish His will in you; and believe that the surest path is to act this way in all things.
