-> ON PERFECTION. Ch. XX. 201 <-

even though in themselves they might be good. Thus, you must never use these words: “Do you love me dearly?” or “Do you not love me at all?” with your relatives or with anyone else, unless it is for some important purpose or for the spiritual well-being of that person. For it may happen that, in order to prepare one of your brothers, your relatives, or any similar person to listen to a truth and benefit from it, there will be a need to use these expressions of affection that are so pleasing to the senses. It may even be that one of these “obliging words” <note>original: "paroles obligeantes"; these are the polite, flattering, or affectionate social pleasantries common in high society, which Teresa views as potentially hollow.</note> (for that is what they are called in the world) will have a greater effect on their mind than many other words spoken purely in the language of God <note>This refers to speaking strictly in scriptural or theological terms without social "softening."</note>; and as a result of this preparation, those spiritual truths will touch them much more deeply than they would have otherwise. Thus, provided that you use such language only with this goal and intention, I do not disapprove of it. But otherwise, these words bring no profit and could even cause harm without you even noticing it.

<summary>Teresa advises the nuns to avoid idle affectionate talk unless it serves as a tool to open a relative's heart to spiritual truth. She argues that while worldly pleasantries can be useful for preparing someone to hear the Gospel, they are otherwise useless or harmful to a religious life.</summary>

102          THE WAY

Do the people of the world not know that, as Religious <note>original: "Religieuses"; women who have taken vows in a religious order, such as the Carmelites.</note> sisters, your occupation is Prayer <note>original: "Oraiſon"; specifically referring to mental or contemplative prayer, the core of the Carmelite life.</note>? On this point, take great care not to say: “I do not want to pass for good in their minds,” since, forming as you do a part of the Community, all the good or all the evil they notice in you will also fall back upon it. It is undoubtedly a great evil that persons who, being Religious, are so particularly obliged to speak only of God, imagine they can reasonably dissimulate on such occasions, unless it were for some great good: which happens only very rarely. This must be your manner of acting: this must be your language. Let those who wish to deal with you learn it, then, if it seems good to them: and if they do not, take great care not to learn theirs, which would be for you the path to hell. And if they take you for coarse and uncivil, what does it matter to you if they hold that belief? If for hypocrites, even less. You will gain by being visited only

## CHAPTER XXI.

*That in the path of <term>Prayer: "Oraison" in the original French, referring to mental or contemplative prayer—the silent, interior conversation with God that is the hallmark of the Carmelite tradition.</term> nothing should prevent one from always moving forward. Despising all the fears that people try to instill regarding the difficulties and perils encountered there. That sometimes one or two persons raised up by God to make the truth known prevail over many others joined together to obscure and combat it.*

<margin>That one must walk without fear on the path of Prayer.</margin>
Let the number of things through which one must pass to undertake this divine journey, and to enter upon this royal road <note>The "Royal Road" (camino real) is a central metaphor in Teresa’s writing, signifying the direct, though disciplined, path to God through prayer.</note> that leads to Heaven, not astonish you at all, my Daughters. Is it strange that, when it comes to acquiring such a great treasure, it seems at first that it must cost us very dearly? A time will come when we shall know that the whole world together would not be enough to pay for it.

To return then to the manner in which those who must begin...

<summary>Saint Teresa introduces Chapter XXI, encouraging her nuns to embark on the "royal road" of mental prayer without fear of the difficulties or the discouragement offered by others. She emphasizes that the spiritual treasure found at the end of the journey is of infinite value, far outweighing any initial cost or effort.</summary>
<keywords>The Way of Perfection, mental prayer, spiritual journey, courage, royal road, Saint Teresa of Avila, monastic discipline</keywords>

...who wish to enter upon this path, and to walk always until they arrive at the source of this water of life to drink from it and to satisfy themselves; I say that it is of the utmost importance to have a firm resolution to never stop until one reaches the fountain, whatever difficulty may arise, whatever obstacle one may encounter, whatever murmur <note>In this context, "murmurs" refers to the discouraging gossip or critical opinions of others who might judge someone's spiritual dedication.</note> one might hear, whatever pain one might suffer, whatever fortune one might run, whatever appearance there may be of being unable to resist so many labors, and finally even if one believes they must die from it, and though the whole world should be swallowed up in an abyss. ### OF PERFECTION. Ch. XXI. 207

...[they] should suffice them. I agree, my Sisters, that they should suffice them; and why do they not suffice them, since one could not fail in basing one’s Prayer <term>Prayer: "Oraiſon" in the original French, referring to "mental prayer"—a silent, internal conversation with God rather than the rote recitation of words.</term> upon that which came from the mouth of Jesus Christ Himself? They are undoubtedly right; and if our weakness were not so great, and our devotion so cold, we would have no need of other Prayers, nor of any Books to instruct us in prayer.

This is why, since I am speaking to persons who cannot recollect themselves <note>In this tradition, "recollection" (recueillement) is a specific spiritual practice of withdrawing the senses and thoughts from the outside world to focus on the divine presence within the soul.</note> by applying themselves to meditate on other Mysteries which seem to them too subtle and too refined, and since there are spirits so delicate that nothing is capable of satisfying them; I deem it appropriate to establish here certain principles, certain means, and certain intentions for Prayer, without stopping at things that are too elevated. Thus, your Books cannot be taken from you <note>This is likely a reference to the historical context of the 16th century, when the Inquisition banned many spiritual books written in the common tongue (the vernacular); Teresa argues that the Lord's Prayer is an internal book that no authority can seize.</note>, since provided that you apply your affection to this and are humble, you will have no need of anything more. I will...

208 THE WAY

...I am always very attached; and the words of the Gospel make me enter into a greater recollection <note>As noted on the previous page, "recollection" is the spiritual practice of withdrawing the mind from external distractions to focus on the internal presence of God.</note> than the most learned and best-written works, especially when the authors are not strongly approved <note>Teresa lived during the Spanish Inquisition; "approved" authors were those whose works had been vetted by the Church to be free of heresy. It is not that I pretend to give you a full explanation of these divine prayers <note>Referring to the foundational prayers of the Church, primarily the "Our Father."</note>. Plenty of others have done so; and even if that were not the case, I would not be so bold as to undertake it, knowing well that it would be folly. # ON PERFECTION. Ch. XXI. 209

...who doubts that this divine Master acts similarly toward us?

Therefore, mock all those fears that people will try to give you, and all those perils with which they will want to frighten you. For since the road that leads to the possession of a great treasure is entirely full of robbers, what hope is there of claiming to be able to pass through it without danger? Would the people of the world suffer their treasures to be taken from them without opposition—they who, for an interest of nothingness <note>That is, for trivial or worldly gains that amount to nothing in eternity.</note>, spend entire nights without sleep and kill both body and soul?

If, therefore, when you go to acquire—or better said, to seize this treasure by force, according to that word of Our Lord: *that the violent take it by force* <note>original: "que les violens le ravissent." A reference to Matthew 11:12. In the 17th-century spiritual context, this "violence" refers to the holy fervor and resolute determination needed to overcome one's own nature and reach God.</note>. ### OF PERFECTION. Ch. XXI. 211

It is not a question now of knowing whether this **Prayer** <term>Prayer: Here, Teresa uses the term *Oraiſon*, which specifically refers to "Mental Prayer"—an internal, heart-to-heart conversation with God, as opposed to simply reciting set prayers.</term> should be mental for some, and vocal for others; I say only that you have need of both. This is the exercise of Religious persons <note>Religious persons: Those who have taken vows in a monastic order, such as the nuns for whom Teresa wrote this manual.</note>: and whoever tells you that there is danger in it, consider him as being, through this bad advice he gives you, so perilous a reef for you, that if you do not avoid him by fleeing, he will cause you to be shipwrecked.

Engrave this advice, I pray you, in your memory, since you might have need of it. The danger would be to lack humility and to not possess the other virtues. But God forbid <note>original: *à Dieu ne plaiſe*</note> that one could ever say there is danger in the path of Prayer. There is great reason to believe that these fears are an invention of the devil, who uses this artifice to cause the fall of some souls who devote themselves to Prayer.

### CHAPTER XXII.

*On Mental Prayer. That it must*
*always be joined to vocal prayer.*
*On the infinite perfections of God.*

</margin>

KNOW, my Daughters, that the difference in Prayer <note>original: *Oraison*. In the 17th century, this term referred to a formal, structured period of prayer or a specific discourse with God.</note> should not be determined by our voice or our words, such that when we speak it is vocal, and when we remain silent it is mental. For if, while praying vocally, I occupy myself entirely with the consideration that I am speaking to God; if I keep myself in His presence; and if I am more attentive to this consideration than to the words themselves that I pronounce, it is then that mental and vocal Prayer are joined together. Unless one wished to make us believe that one speaks to God when, in pronouncing the Our Father <note>original: *Pater*. The Latin name for the Lord’s Prayer, derived from its opening words, *Pater Noster*.</note>, one thinks of the world; in which case I have nothing to say. But if, in speaking to so great a Lord <note>original: *Seigneur*. A title emphasizing God's sovereignty and majesty, reflecting the courtly language of 16th-century Spain.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa defines mental prayer as the internal awareness of God's presence, arguing that vocal prayer—like reciting the Our Father—only becomes true prayer when the mind is focused on the Divine rather than worldly distractions.</summary>

...to remain alone with God: and I pray that this may suffice to make us understand with whom we are then, and what he deigns to answer to our requests. For do you believe that he remains silent even if we do not hear him? Now, just as the master stays close to his disciple, and never moves so far away that he needs to cry out in a loud voice to make himself heard by him: I desire likewise that you know that to say the Our Father <note>original: "Pater noſter"; the Latin name for the Lord's Prayer.</note> well, you must not distance yourself from this divine Master who taught you to say it.

I agree that what I have just proposed can be called <term>mental prayer</term> <note>Oraiſon mentale: A form of prayer characterized by internal dialogue or meditation on God, rather than the mere recitation of set formulas.</note>. Therefore, do not let yourselves give way to impatience; but try to accustom yourselves to a thing which is so necessary for you.

If, after having considered him, you do not find him beautiful, I permit you to look at him no longer, even though this celestial Spouse <note>The "Celestial Spouse" refers to Jesus Christ, following the tradition of "Bridal Mysticism" where the soul or the Church is viewed as the bride of the Divine.</note> never ceases to keep his eyes fixed upon you. Alas! although he has suffered a thousand indignities from you, he does not cease to look at you: and would you think it a great effort if you turned your gaze from external things to cast it sometimes upon him?

Consider, as the Bride says in the Song of Songs <note>The "Cantique" refers to the *Song of Songs* or *Canticle of Canticles*, a book of the Old Testament often interpreted by mystics like Teresa as an allegory for the love between God and the soul.</note>, that he desires nothing else but that we look at him. Thus, provided that you seek him, you will find him as you desire him. For he takes so much pleasure in seeing us fix our sight upon him, that there is nothing he would not do to lead us to it.

It is said that for women to live well with their husbands, they must follow all their sentiments, showing sadness when they are sad, and joy when they are cheerful, even though they may have none in their hearts: (this

This is truly, and without any exaggeration, how Our Lord deals with us: for he wishes us to be the mistresses: he subjects himself to our desires, and conforms himself to our feelings. Thus, if you are in joy, consider him resurrected: and then what contentment will be yours to see him come forth from the tomb, all radiant with perfections, all brilliant with Majesty, all resplendent with light, and all filled with the pleasure that the winning of a bloody battle gives to a victor, making him master of a great Kingdom which he has conquered solely to give to you. Could you, after that, believe it is too much to do to cast your eyes sometimes upon him who thus wishes to put the scepter in your hand and the crown upon your head?

250      THE WAY

pleasure in speaking with him, not through studied speeches, but with simple words that show him how much you are moved by what he suffers. O my Savior and my God, is it possible that you do not disdain the company of such a lowly creature <note>The original French *vile creature* reflects the common language of 16th-century mysticism, where the author emphasizes their own smallness in comparison to the greatness of God.</note> as I am? OF PERFECTION <note>Original: *De la Perfection*, referring to *The Way of Perfection*.</note> Ch. XXVI. 255

...ercising yourself in this, you will be able to pass from one stage to the next.

To help you in this, choose from among the **Images of Our Lord** the one that inspires the most devotion in you—not simply to carry it on your person without ever looking at it, but to remind you to speak often to him; and he will not fail to put into your heart and into your mouth what you should say to him. Since you speak easily to other people, how could words fail you when conversing with God? Do not believe such a thing, my Sisters. For my part, I cannot believe that this could happen, provided that you practice it. For if you do not, who can doubt that words will fail you? When we stop conversing with someone, they become like a stranger to us, even if they are a close relative; we no longer know what to say to them, because kinship and friendship vanish when communication ceases. <note>Teresa uses the analogy of human relationships to explain that prayer is essentially a "friendship" that requires regular contact to stay alive.</note>

Therefore, always remain close to this divine Master <note>The "Master" refers to Jesus Christ, whom Teresa presents as the ultimate teacher of prayer.</note> with a very great desire to learn what He will teach you. He will undoubtedly make you good disciples, and will not abandon you unless you abandon Him yourselves. Consider all His words attentively. The first ones He utters will make you know the extreme love He bears for you. And what can be sweeter or more agreeable to a good disciple than to see that their Master loves them?

264 THE WAY [OF PERFECTION]

...[one] would hardly doubt <note>The sentence starts mid-thought from the previous page, likely concluding that a soul who understands God's presence would have difficulty believing that true glory exists anywhere other than where His Majesty is found.</note> that all glory is found wherever His eternal Majesty resides.

Consider what Saint Augustine says: that after having sought God in every place, he found Him within himself. <note>Teresa refers to a famous passage in Saint Augustine’s *Confessions* (Book 10, Chapter 27): "Late have I loved Thee... you were within me, and I was in the world outside."</note> Do you think it is of little use for a soul that is distracted to understand this truth, and to know that she has no need to go to Heaven to speak to her divine Father, or to find all her joy in Him, nor to cry out with all her strength to converse with Him? 

He is so close to us that even if we speak only in a whisper, He does not fail to hear us; and we have no need of wings to rise toward Him. It is enough to keep ourselves in solitude, to look at Him within ourselves, and never to distance ourselves from the company of such a divine guest. <note>original: "divin hoſte." -> OF PERFECTION. Ch. XXVIII. 267 <-

recollection, because the soul there gathers all its powers <note>In the mystical tradition, these "powers" or faculties are the memory, understanding, and will.    Those who are able to shut themselves in, as I have just said, within this little Heaven of our soul where they find Him who is its Creator as well as the earth's—and who accustom themselves to look at nothing outside of there, and to not place themselves in a situation where their external senses might be distracted—must believe that they are walking on an excellent path, and that by advancing much in a short time, they will soon drink from the water of the celestial fountain. It even happens that once this has occurred several times in this manner, Our Lord makes them stop entirely without further hindering the soul from entering into a perfect contemplation. ### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XXVIII. 275

...may place and remove everything that pleases Him, as something belonging absolutely to Him.

This is what His Divine Majesty desires from us; and since there is nothing more reasonable, could we refuse it to Him? He does not wish to force our will; He receives what it gives Him. But He only gives Himself entirely to us when we give ourselves entirely to Him. This is certain and so important that I cannot repeat it too much. 

This eternal King only acts fully within our soul when He sees it free from everything and belonging completely to Him. Could He act otherwise, since He perfectly loves order? And if we were to fill this palace with common folk drawn from the dregs of the people <note>The author uses "lie du peuple" (dregs of the people) as a metaphor for base desires and worldly distractions that are unworthy of the divine presence.</note> and with all sorts of trifles <note>In this context, *bagatelles* refers to worthless trinkets or idle, vain thoughts that clutter the mind.</note>, how could so great a Prince come to lodge there with His entire Court? Would it not be quite enough if He merely wished to remain for a few moments in the midst of such clutter? <note>original: *embarras*, meaning obstruction, confusion, or a crowd of people causing a blockage.</note> 

For do you think, my daughters <note>St. Teresa of Avila is addressing her "daughters," the sisters of the Discalced Carmelite order, for whom she wrote this instruction manual on prayer.</note>, that this King of Glory comes alone? Do you not understand that His Son, after...

In conclusion, I say that anyone who desires to form this habit—for it is one that depends on our own efforts—must not grow weary of accustoming themselves to gradually becoming master of themselves by recalling their senses back to their presence. This is not a loss for the soul, but a great gain; since by cutting off the external use of its senses, it makes them serve its interior recollection. Thus, if we speak, let us try to remember that in the depths of our heart we have someone to speak with; if we hear someone else speaking, let us remember that we must listen to the One who speaks to us from much closer within; and that, finally, we should always consider that we can—

not disagreeable; especially if it concerns a matter of such consequence as that which Our Savior teaches us to ask: and this seems to me very significant.

—blind, and we have such a distaste for that which can give us life, that we love only that which can give us death—and a death that is not only terrifying, but eternal.

    Now, to ask God that it might please Him to establish His Kingdom within us, Our Lord commands us to say these words: *Hallowed be Thy Name, and Thy Kingdom come.* <note>The original French reads "que voſtre regne nous arrive," emphasizing the arrival or approach of the Kingdom to the soul.</note> See, my Daughters, the infinite wisdom of our Master. It is here that I reflect, and it is important to reflect, upon what we are asking for when we request this Kingdom. As our Savior knows that in our extreme helplessness we are incapable of worthily hallowing, praising, and glorifying this adorable Name of the eternal Father, unless His supreme Majesty gives us the means by giving us His Kingdom here, He wished, in the petitions He made to Him for us, to join these two things together.

Does it not seem to you, my Sisters, that I am suggesting that in order to make this request and to pray well vocally, we should be Angels? <note>St. Teresa often addresses her "Sisters," the Discalced Carmelite nuns for whom she wrote this manual of prayer.</note> Certainly our divine Master would wish it, since He commands us to make such an elevated request, and He surely does not oblige us to ask for things that are impossible. <margin>Regarding the Prayer of Quiet, which is pure contemplation.</margin>

I want then, my Daughters, to tell you what this Prayer of Quiet is, according to what I have heard said of it, and how Our Lord has made me understand it, perhaps so that I may instruct you in it. It is, in my opinion, in this Prayer that He begins to let us know that our requests are pleasing to Him, and that He wishes—starting here below—to make us enter into the possession of His Kingdom, so that we may praise Him, that we may sanctify Him, and that we may work with all our power to ensure that others praise and sanctify Him. ...acquire it, regardless of the care we might bring to the task. For it is to place our soul in peace and in calm, or rather, it is to feel that Our Lord places it there by His divine presence, establishing all its <term>faculties and powers</term> <note>In the psychology of the time, the "faculties" of the soul usually referred to the memory, understanding, and will.</note> in a state of complete rest, just as we see in the Gospel that He acted in such a manner toward Simeon the Just <note>A reference to Luke 2:25–35, where the aged Simeon recognizes the infant Jesus in the Temple as the Messiah. When the soul is in this state, it understands—in a way very different from that which occurs through the mediation of our external senses—that it is already close to its God, and that if it approaches but a little more, it will become one and the same thing with Him by means of <term>union</term>.

It is not that the soul sees this, neither with the eyes of the body nor with the eyes of the soul, any more than Saint Simeon saw the divine JESUS as anything other than the appearance of a simple child; and judging by the manner in which He was clothed and wrapped, and by the small number of people who followed Him, one might rather have taken Him for the son of some poor man.

He replied to her that she should not be surprised by it, and that the same happened to him. This gives me reason to believe that since the soul is so fully satisfied in this <term>Prayer of Quietude<note>The "Prayer of Quietude" is a stage of mystical prayer where the soul experiences an internal peace and a sense of God's presence, though the mind may still be somewhat active.</note></term>, there is a strong appearance that, most often, the will finds itself united to Him who alone is capable of filling it with happiness. 

And because there are some among you whom Our Lord, by His sole goodness, has favored with this grace, it seems to me that it will not be out of place for me to give them some advice on this subject.

The first [error] is that when souls enjoy this consolation without knowing how it has come to them—recognizing only that they neither contributed to it nor could have contributed—they fall into the temptation of believing it is in their power to maintain themselves in this state. This results in them hardly daring to breathe <note>Teresa refers to a common spiritual anxiety where a person becomes physically and mentally stiff, fearing that any movement will break the "spell" of the divine connection.</note>. But this is a fantasy <note>original: "reſverie." In this context, it implies a delusion or a mistaken belief.</note>. For just as we can neither make the day arrive nor prevent the night from coming, we can no more procure for ourselves so great a favor as this <term>Prayer<note>Here, Teresa refers specifically to the "Prayer of Quietude," a state of contemplative rest that she considers a gift from God rather than a result of human effort.</note></term>, nor prevent it from passing away. It is an entirely supernatural thing: we have no part in it, and we are so incapable of acquiring it by our own strength that the way to enjoy it longer is to recognize that, being utterly unworthy of deserving it, we can neither hasten nor delay its departure, but only receive it with great acts of thanksgiving. And these acts of thanksgiving do not consist of a multitude of words, but rather in imitating the <term>Publican<note>A reference to the Parable of the Pharisee and the Publican (Luke 18:9–14). <summary>Saint Teresa explains that the Prayer of Quietude is a supernatural gift that cannot be controlled or sustained by human effort. She warns against the anxiety of trying to "hold onto" the state, advocating instead for humble gratitude.</summary>

    Solitude <note>The original French "la retraite" implies both a physical withdrawal and an internal state of seclusion where the soul makes space for the divine.</note> can then be very useful to leave the place entirely free for Our Lord, so that His sovereign Majesty may dispose, in whatever manner He pleases, of a creature who belongs entirely to Him.    Therefore, when the <term>will<note>The "will" is the faculty of the soul that loves and chooses; in this state, it is resting in God's presence.</note></term> finds itself in this tranquility and in this quietude, it should no more pay attention to the <term>understanding<note>The "understanding" or "intellect" is the faculty of reason. The result would be that not only would the soul fail to gain any greater profit from its <term>Prayer<note>Teresa uses "Oraison" specifically to refer to "mental prayer," a deep, internal conversation with the Divine.</note></term> by this means, but all its efforts would only serve to make it lose what God had given it without the soul having contributed anything of its own.

   Here is a comparison that Our Lord put into my mind one day during Prayer, which in my opinion explains this very clearly; for this reason, I pray you to consider it well. The soul in this state resembles a child who is still nursing; its mother, to caress it while it is in her arms, lets the milk flow into its mouth without the child so much as moving its lips.

—s. <note>original: "es". In this way, she enters into a happy forgetting of herself through the confidence that He, in whose presence she is so happy to find herself, will provide for all her needs. Whereas, if she were to stop to argue with the understanding—trying to make it share in her happiness against its will by pulling it after her by force—it would necessarily happen that, being unable to pay strong attention to different things at once, she would let this milk spill, and would thus find herself deprived of this divine nourishment.

<meta>This page continues the comparison of states of mystical prayer in Chapter XXXI. 306 THE WAY [OF PERFECTION]

<margin>Difference between the Prayer of Quiet and the Prayer of Union.</margin>

Now, there is this difference between the <term>Prayer of Quiet<note>A state of contemplative prayer where the soul experiences God's peace but can still be distracted by the wandering mind.</note></term> and that in which the soul is entirely united to God: in this latter state, the soul does not receive this divine nourishment like food that enters the mouth before passing into the stomach; rather, she finds it all at once within herself, without knowing in what manner Our Lord has placed it there. On the other hand, in the first state, it seems that God wants the soul to work a little, though she does so with such sweetness that she hardly notices her labor. Any trouble she may experience then comes from her <term>understanding<note>The "understanding" refers to the reasoning or analytical part of the mind, which Teresa often contrasts with the "will," the part of the soul that loves.</note></term> or from her imagination. This does not happen in that other, more perfect <term>Prayer<note>original: "Oraison". In this context, it refers to a state of internal communion with God rather than spoken words.</note></term> where all three <term>faculties<note>In 16th-century theology, the soul was thought to have three "powers" or faculties: the Will (which loves), the Understanding (which reasons), and the Memory.</note></term> are found united, because He who created them then suspends them; the pleasure with which He fills them is so great that they are entirely occupied by it, without being able to comprehend how this is happening.

When the soul finds herself in this <term>Prayer of Union<note>A deeper mystical state than "Quiet" where the mind, memory, and will are all focused entirely on God, making distraction impossible.</note></term>, she feels clearly that the will enjoys a contentment...

<summary>Saint Teresa distinguishes between the "Prayer of Quiet," where the mind can still be distracted, and the "Prayer of Union," where God "suspends" all mental faculties in a state of total absorption.</summary>

### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XXXI. 311

But there are people, among whose number I was once counted, whose hearts Our Lord softens, to whom He inspires holy resolutions, makes known the vanity of all worldly things, and finally gives His Kingdom by placing them in this Prayer of Quiet <note>The "Prayer of Quiet" (*Oraison de quiétude*) is a state of contemplative prayer described by St. Teresa where the soul feels a deep, peaceful intimacy with God, and the will is resting in Him.</note>, who yet make themselves deaf to His voice; this is because they are so intent on saying very hurriedly—as if to finish a task—the quantity of vocal prayers <note>Spoken prayers with a set form, such as the Rosary or the Office, as opposed to spontaneous mental prayer.</note> they have resolved to recite each day. Even though Our Lord, as I have just said, places His Kingdom in their hands, they do not wish to receive it; but imagining they do better by praying in this other manner, they lose the attention they should pay to such a great favor.

In the name of God, my Daughters <note>St. Teresa addresses her audience of Discalced Carmelite nuns.</note>, do not conduct yourselves in this way; but watch over yourselves when it pleases Him to grant you such a grace. Consider that it would be to lose a very great treasure through your own fault, and that it is doing much more to say from time to time some word of the *Pater* <note>original: "Pater" — The *Pater Noster* or "Our Father" prayer.</note>,

<margin>Concerning the prayer of rapture.</margin> Oh my Daughters, how great is the advantage we receive for having made this gift! It is such that, provided we offer it with all our heart, it can cause the Most High to unite Himself with our lowliness, transform us into Himself <note>Teresa refers here to the "Unitive Way," the highest stage of mystical life where the soul is spiritually married to God and its will becomes indistinguishable from the Divine will.</note>, and thus make the Creator and the creature one and the same thing. See then, I pray you, if you will be well rewarded; and how great is the goodness of this divine Master, who, knowing by what means one can make oneself pleasing to His Father, teaches us what we must do to please Him and to gain this affection. The more we incline ourselves with a full will to fulfill our duties to Him, and show by our actions that the assurances we give Him are not feigned <note>Saint Teresa frequently warns that spiritual progress requires practical virtue and "works," not just "sweet feelings" or empty words during prayer.</note>; the more He draws us near to Himself and detaches us from all the things of the earth and from ourselves, in order to make us capable of receiving such great and such precious <note>The sentence ends mid-thought and likely continues on the next page with a word like "favors" or "graces."</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that the total surrender of the will leads to a mystical union where the soul is transformed into God. she emphasizes that God rewards sincere, active devotion by detaching the soul from earthly desires.</summary>
<keywords>surrender of will, mystical union, prayer of rapture, transformation, spiritual reward, detachment, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

--- Page 351 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Unitive Way" from page 324. The sentence began on the previous page with "receiving such great and such precious" and concludes here with "favors."</meta>

->OF PERFECTION. Ch. XXXII. 325<-

favors. For this proof of the love we bear for Him is so pleasing to Him, that He does not cease to reward us in this life, and brings us to the point where we no longer know what to ask of Him, without Him ever growing weary of giving to us. Thus, not content with having made us one and the same thing with Himself by uniting us to Him, He begins to take His delights in us, to reveal His secrets to us, and to rejoice that we recognize our own happiness—that we see, though obscurely <note>The "obscure" vision refers to the "cloud of unknowing" or the "dark night," where the soul perceives God through faith rather than clear sight.</note>, what joys He reserves for us in the next life. Finally, He causes all our outer senses to vanish in such a way that there is nothing left but He alone who occupies our mind.

This is what is called **rapture** <note>original: "ravissement." In Teresian spirituality, rapture is a state of ecstasy where the soul's faculties are suspended and entirely absorbed in God.</note>: and it is then that God begins to show such great friendship to this soul, and to deal so familiarly with it, that not only does He return its will, but He gives it His own; and He goes so far as to take pleasure in its commanding His

<summary>Saint Teresa describes the state of mystical rapture, where God rewards the soul's surrender by revealing divine secrets and uniting His own will with the will of the practitioner.</summary>
<keywords>rapture, mystical union, divine favors, Saint Teresa of Avila, surrender of will, spiritual ecstasy</keywords>

As for the poor soul, though she may wish, she cannot do what she wants. She cannot even will, unless God gives her this will; and her greatest wealth consists in this: that the more she serves Him, the more she is indebted to Him. It even often happens that, wishing to pay some of what she owes, she torments and afflicts herself to see herself subject to so many obligations, embarrassments, and bonds that the prison of her body <note>A common metaphor in mystical literature for the physical self and its worldly needs, which are seen as a hindrance to total spiritual union.</note> drags along with it. But she is quite foolish to torment herself over it, since even though we may do everything within our power, how would it be possible for us to pay any part of what we owe Him? For we have, as I have said, nothing to give to God except what

Thus, after having recognized with humility the helplessness in which we find ourselves by our own means, we ought only to think of perfectly accomplishing that which we can do through His grace, which is to consecrate to Him all of our will. Everything else does nothing but embarrass <note>In this 17th-century context, "embarrass" (French: *embarrasser*) means to clutter, tangle, or impede the soul's progress, rather than simply meaning social awkwardness.</note> a soul that He has placed in this state, and harms her rather than serves her.

Understand well, I pray you, my Sisters, that I say this only for those souls whom Our Lord has willed to unite to Himself by a perfect union and contemplation. For in that state, it is humility alone that can achieve anything—and not a humility acquired by the understanding <note>Teresa distinguishes between "acquired" humility (humility practiced through human reasoning and effort) and "infused" humility (a direct gift from God that bypasses the intellect).</note>, but a humility proceeding from the clear light of truth. This light gives us, in an instant, a knowledge of our own nothingness <note>Teresa often uses the concept of "nothingness" (Spanish: *nada*) to describe the state of the creature in the presence of the Creator.</note> and of the infinite greatness of God—a realization that our imagination, even with great labor, could not acquire over a long period of time.

I add here a piece of advice: you must not imagine that you can arrive at this happiness by your [own efforts...]

...we should not fail to give Him our will, because He knows that otherwise we would have great difficulty in resolving ourselves to do so, even though it is so advantageous for us to give it to Him that our entire happiness consists in this point. For if one says to a wealthy person devoted to pleasure <note>original: "riche voluptueux." ON PERFECTION Ch. XXXIV. 337

I shall have no trouble agreeing to it, since it is always a great enough task for me to take it upon myself to express my feelings on such a subject. It seems to me, then, that he speaks thus to let us know that we shall not possess him only on earth, but that we shall possess him also in Heaven, if we know how to benefit from the happiness of being here below in his company; since he remains with us only to sustain us, help us, and encourage us, so that, as I have said, the will of his Father may be fulfilled in us.

This word "today" <note>original: "aujourd'huy"; Saint Teresa is interpreting the phrase "Give us this day" from the Lord's Prayer.</note>, in my opinion, represents the entire duration of the world, which, to speak truly, should only be considered as a single day—especially for those wretched souls who damn themselves <note>In the theology of the time, "damnation" referred to the eternal separation of the soul from God, a concept Teresa treats with profound gravity.</note>, since there will be no more "day" for them in the next life, but only eternal darkness. Now, it is not the fault of Our Lord if they allow themselves to be overcome. For he encourages them unceasingly until the end of the battle, without them being able to either excuse themselves or complain to the Eternal Father—

### ->ON PERFECTION Ch. XXXIV.<- 349

...present ourselves before one of His **Images**, it seems to me that when we have Him Himself present within us, it would be a folly to leave Him to address His painting; just as it would be, without doubt, if having the portrait of a person we loved extremely well, and that person coming to see us, we left them without saying a word to go and talk with that portrait.

But do you know at what time this is no less useful than holy, and when I take a very great pleasure in it? It is when Our Lord distances Himself from us, and makes us know His absence through the **dryness** <note>In mystical theology, "dryness" (*sécheresse*) refers to a period where a believer feels no emotional or spiritual satisfaction or "warmth" during prayer, often viewed as a trial to test the soul's devotion.</note> in which He leaves us. Then it is such a consolation to me to consider the portrait of Him whom I have so much reason to love; that I would wish to never be able to turn my eyes without seeing Him. For upon what holier or more agreeable object can we rest our sight than upon Him who has so much love for us, and who is the principle and the source of all good things? Oh, how miserable are those **heretics** <note>Saint Teresa is writing during the height of the Protestant Reformation; here she refers to those who rejected the use of religious images, icons, and paintings in worship, which she considers a great spiritual loss.</note> who have lost through their own fault this...

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that while religious images are secondary when Christ is physically present in the soul during Communion, they are vital comforts during times of spiritual dryness. She criticizes the rejection of these images by contemporary religious reformers.</summary>

### 350 THE WAY

...consolation and so many others!

Since, therefore, after having received the most holy Eucharist you have within you **JESUS CHRIST** Himself, close the eyes of the body to open the eyes of the soul, so that you may look at Him in the center of your heart. For I have already told you, I tell you again, and I would wish to say it unceasingly: that if you accustom yourselves to this every time you have received Communion, and strive to keep your conscience so pure that you are permitted to enjoy such great happiness often; this divine Spouse <note>Saint Teresa frequently uses the metaphor of the "Spouse" (*Epoux*) to describe the intimate, mystical relationship between Christ and the soul.</note> will not disguise Himself in such a way that He does not make Himself known to you in various ways, in proportion to the desire you have to know Him: and this desire may even be such that He reveals Himself entirely to your soul.

### *ON PERFECTION Ch. XXXIV. 351*

...then make Himself known to us? Certainly not, since when He showed Himself openly to men and told them clearly who He was, they treated Him so badly <note>original: "si-mal." The author refers to the rejection and Passion of Christ during His earthly life.</note>, and so few believed in Him. It is quite enough of a favor that He does for all of us to wish that we know it is He Himself who is present in this adorable Sacrament.

But He does not reveal Himself, nor does He share His greatness and His treasures, except to those whom He knows desire Him with ardor, because only they are His true friends. Thus, he who is not so fortunate as to be His friend, and to approach Him to receive Him after having done everything in his power to make himself worthy, imports Him in vain to make Himself known to him. These sorts of people, when they go to the holy table <note>The "holy table" refers to the altar or communion rail where the Eucharist is received.</note> once a year, have such impatience to have satisfied the commandment of the Church <note>This refers to the "Easter Duty," the minimum ecclesiastical requirement for Catholics to receive Communion at least once a year during the Easter season.</note>, that they chase **JESUS-CHRIST** out of themselves as soon as He has entered; or to put it better, the affairs, the occupations, and the troubles of the world <term>the world: "le siècle" in original French. In this spiritual context, it refers to secular life and mundane distractions rather than a period of time.</term>

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that Christ remains hidden in the Eucharist to those who lack fervent desire or only receive Communion out of obligation, as they quickly distract themselves with worldly affairs.</summary>

For just as in winter, if we entered a room where there was a great fire, even if we stayed far away instead of approaching it, we could not warm ourselves well; yet this would not prevent us from feeling less cold than if there were no fire at all. # CHAPTER XXXVI.

->**On these words of the Our Father: *And forgive us our debts as we forgive those who have offended us.* Whereupon the Saint dwells at length to show what folly it is to dwell on petty points of honor in Monasteries.**<-

<margin>On these words of the Our Father: And forgive us our debts.</margin>

**OUR** divine Master, seeing that this heavenly food <note>original: "viande celeste." In the 17th century, "viande" referred to food or sustenance in general, not specifically meat. Here, it refers to the Eucharist.</note> makes all things so easy for us, and that provided our sins do not place any obstacle in the way, we can carry out what we have said to His Father—that His will be done in us—He adds: *and forgive us our debts as we forgive those who have offended us.* On this point, I pray you consider, my Sisters, that He does not say, "as we *will* forgive"; <note>The Saint highlights the present tense of the verb to emphasize that forgiveness must be an immediate and ongoing state of heart for the Christian.</note> this is to make us understand that he who comes to ask the eternal Father for a gift as precious as the sacred bread of the body of His Son <note>original: "le pain sacré du corps de son [Fils]." Son <note>The text continues from the previous page: "the sacred bread of the body of His Son."</note>, & who has perfectly submitted his will to that of God, must have already forgiven others everything they might have committed against him. Consider how the Saints rejoiced to suffer persecutions and insults, because these gave them the means to offer something to God at the same time they were asking so many things of Him. But what shall a poor sinner such as I am do, having had so few occasions to forgive, and having so great a need to be forgiven? ## ON PERFECTION Ch. XXXVI. 365

In what, then, my divine Master, have You established Your honor? Did You lose it by humbling Yourself even unto death? No, certainly not; but on the contrary, this debasement <note>original: "abaissement." This refers to the theological concept of *kenosis*, or the "self-emptying" of Christ in becoming human and submitting to the cross.</note> was the cause and the source of the honor of all men. 

Alas! my Daughters, I ask you in the name of God to consider that if we take this path <note>The path of seeking social status or seniority within the convent.</note>, we shall never arrive where we intend to go, since we will lose our way from the very start. And I pray with all my heart to Our Lord that no soul may be lost through this detestable "point of honor" <note>original: "point d'honneur." In 16th-century society, this was a rigid code of reputation and social standing. Teresa argues that such pride has no place in a monastery.</note> without even knowing what it consists of. 

What! For having forgiven things that were, in effect, neither an injury, nor an affront, nor anything at all, shall we believe we have done something considerable? And shall we imagine that God must forgive us, because we have forgiven? 

Bring light, Lord, into the darkness of our ignorance; make us know that we do not know ourselves; that we present ourselves to You with empty hands, and forgive—

In Teresian spirituality, this specifically refers to "Mental Prayer," an internal and silent heart-to-heart communication with God.</note> or this contemplation of which I have spoken, although one may notice in them other imperfections and other faults: nevertheless, regarding the forgiveness of offenses, I have never seen them fail in it, nor do I believe they could, if these favors truly come from God.

   This is why the greater these favors are, the more those who receive them must take care to see if they produce these good effects: and if they produce none, they should greatly fear and believe that they do not come from God, since He never approaches a soul without enriching it by establishing it in virtue. For it is certain that although these favors pass quickly, one knows it over time by the advantages and the good effects that remain in the soul: And thus, as our divine...

<summary>Saint Teresa argues that true divine favors, such as contemplation and mental prayer, are always accompanied by the growth of virtue, particularly the ability to forgive others. She warns that spiritual experiences that do not lead to such moral improvements may be illusions rather than genuine encounters with God.</summary>
<keywords>forgiveness, mental prayer, contemplation, spiritual discernment, supernatural grace, virtue, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

# CHAPTER XXXVII.

## On the excellence of the Prayer of the Our Father, and the advantages to be found in this holy prayer.

[He] has taught in the first four <term>petitions</term> <note>original: "demandes". But in the fifth petition, Our Lord begins to make us known what effects these favors produce in us, when they truly proceed from Him, as I have already said.

   Considering why it might be that **JESUS-CHRIST** did not explain more particularly such obscure and lofty things to make them understood by everyone, it seems to me that it is because this prayer, needing to be general so as to serve everyone, He did not wish to clarify it further; thus, as everyone persuades themselves they understand it well, each person, in saying it, might ask for what is necessary for their consolation and...

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that the first four petitions of the Lord's Prayer contain the entire path of spiritual progress, while the fifth petition reveals the practical effects of divine grace on the soul. ### 376 THE WAY (OF PERFECTION)

Now we are well assured that God will not fail on His part: For was there ever such a good paymaster, and so generous? Our Lord having thus seen how important it is for these souls to be awakened, to make them remember that they have enemies who oblige them to always stand on their guard; and that the more they are elevated, the more they need the help of His eternal Father; since in falling they would fall from higher: And wanting moreover to deliver them from the snares where they...

*Advice for resisting various temptations of the demon, and particularly false humilities, indiscreet penances, and the self-confidence that he inspires in us.*

402      THE WAY

### CHAPTER XL.

That the love and fear of God joined together form a powerful remedy to resist the temptations of the devil. What the misfortune of those who did not love God, and the happiness of those who did love Him, will be at the hour of death.

<margin>Resisting the temptations of the devil through the love and fear of God.</margin>

O My dear Master <note>The author, Saint Teresa of Avila, frequently addresses God or Jesus directly as "Master" or "Lord" throughout her writing, shifting from instruction to prayer.</note>, give us some means to protect ourselves from the snares of our enemies in such a perilous war. The one that His Divine Majesty gives us, my Daughters <note>Saint Teresa wrote this work specifically for her sisters (nuns) in the Discalced Carmelite Order, hence the address "my Daughters."</note>, and which we can use boldly, is to always maintain love and fear. Love will urge us to walk: and fear will make us watch where we walk, so as not to fall on a path where so many things can make us stumble, as happens to almost all those who walk through this life: This will be the true means of not being deceived.

<summary>Saint Teresa introduces the two primary spiritual tools for navigating life's temptations: the love of God, which motivates progress, and the fear of God, which ensures caution.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual warfare, love of God, fear of God, temptation, Saint Teresa of Avila, spiritual guidance</keywords>

--- Page 429 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 402, where Saint Teresa explained that the love and fear of God are the primary tools for avoiding the devil’s deceptions and navigating the spiritual life safely.</meta>

OF PERFECTION <note>Original: "DE LA PERF." This is an abbreviation for the "Way of Perfection," the title of the book.</note> Ch. XL. 403

You will perhaps ask me how you might recognize that you possess these great virtues, and you would be right to ask, since it is certain that you could never be entirely assured of it. For if you were certain that you possessed a true love for God, you would also be certain of being in a state of grace <note>In Catholic theology, particularly during the Counter-Reformation, it was held that an individual could not have absolute certainty of their own salvation or state of grace without a direct revelation from God. This encouraged humility and constant spiritual effort.</note>. There are, however, my Daughters, signs so evident that it seems even the blind could see them. These signs are neither secret nor hidden; they make so much noise that, even if you did not wish to, you could not help but hear them.

The number of those who possess these two qualities in perfection is so small that they are easily noticed because of their rarity; and they are known all the more, the more they remain in silence and secrecy. This love and this fear of God are like two fortresses from which one wages war against the world and the devil. Those who truly love God love everything that is good, desire everything that is good, favor everything that is good, praise everything that is good, and join themselves to [it].

Ll ij <note>This is a "signature mark," a printer's code used in the 17th century to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that while one cannot have absolute certainty of being in a state of grace, the signs of true love and fear of God are so conspicuous that they cannot be hidden. She describes these virtues as spiritual fortresses that naturally incline a person toward everything that is good.</summary>
<keywords>assurance of grace, spiritual discernment, love of God, fear of God, spiritual warfare, virtues, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

--- Page 430 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 403, where Saint Teresa described how those who truly love God are drawn to everything that is good and align themselves with virtuous people.</meta>

404 ->THE WAY<- <note>Original: "LE CHEMIN." This is an abbreviation for "The Way of Perfection."</note>

...always with the good, they support them, defend them, and love only the truth and things worthy of being loved.

For do you believe that those who truly love God could love vanities, or pleasures, or riches, or honors, or any of the other things of the world? Do you believe they could have contentions, disputes, jealousy, and envy? Alas, how could that be possible, since their entire passion is to please the one they love; since they burn with the desire to make themselves worthy of being loved by Him; and since they would give their life with joy if they believed that by this means they could please Him further?

When the love one has for God is genuine, it is impossible to hide it. <margin>On the fear of God.</margin> I come now to that which concerns the fear of God <note>original: "la crainte de Dieu." you a poison that would have contributed to killing your soul, can often help you to love God more, and to thank Him for having delivered you from a peril so harmful to you. Thus, when it is known that a person—however lowly and vile they may be in themselves <note>This reflects the 16th-century spiritual tradition of "contempt of self," where the individual acknowledges their own nothingness in comparison to the greatness of God.</note>—is in a state of grace, and consequently loved by God, they are respected; and one finds it difficult to resolve to give them a displeasure as keen as that which they would receive from seeing [their] offended <note>The sentence ends abruptly; it likely concludes with "their Lord offended" or "the Divine Majesty offended" on the following page.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa explains that a person living in grace exerts a silent, virtuous influence on others, acting as a deterrent to sin and gossip through their mere presence.</summary>

<keywords>grace, virtue, spiritual influence, respect for God, friendship with God, divine majesty</keywords>

OF PERF[ECTION]. Ch. XLI. 421

Lord. I know no other reason for it; but it happens ordinarily.

I exhort you, my Daughters, to flee from unease and constraint <note>original: "la gêne & la contrainte" — in this context, St. Teresa refers to a forced, rigid, or anxious way of practicing religion that lacks interior peace.</note>, because the soul that gives in to them finds itself thereby indisposed toward every kind of good, and sometimes falls into scruples <note>A "scruple" is a technical spiritual term for an irrational or obsessive fear that one has committed a sin where none exists, often leading to paralyzing guilt.</note> which make it useless to itself and to others. For even if, remaining constrained in this way, the soul does not fall into these scruples, although it may be good for itself, it will be incapable of serving others to help them advance in piety, because this constraint is so much an enemy of our nature that it intimidates and frightens us. Thus, although these people may be persuaded that the path you follow is better than the one they walk, the fear of falling into those constraints and rigors where they see you will make them lose the desire they had to enter upon it.

This constraint you might feel would also produce another evil; which is that, seeing others walk by a different path, by dealing freely <note>The sentence is interrupted here; it likely continues to describe how the "constrained" soul might judge those who act with more social freedom or naturalness.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa warns that a rigid, anxious approach to spirituality (constraint) hinders personal growth and discourages others from seeking a religious life, as it goes against human nature.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual freedom, constraint, scruples, piety, influence on others, St. Teresa of Avila</keywords>

### CHAPTER XLII.

*On the final words of the Our Father <note>original: "Pater"</note>:*
*But deliver us from evil.*

**I**t was, it seems to me, with great
reason that the Lord of
our souls made this prayer to His Father: *And*
*deliver us from evil.* That is to say,
deliver us from the perils and the hard-
ships of this life, since we are
constantly at risk of falling. And
for His part, He showed clearly enough how
weary He was of living when He said during
the Last Supper to His Apostles: *With all*
*my heart I have desired to share this Supper with*
*you.* <note>A reference to Luke 22:15, where Jesus expresses His longing for the Passover before His passion.</note> For since this Supper was the last
He was to have, it appears clearly enough by
this how welcome the death He was about to suffer
was to Him. And yet today, those
who are a hundred years old, not only
do not grow weary of living, but
would very much like never to die.

<summary>St. Teresa begins her commentary on the final petition of the Lord's Prayer, explaining that "deliver us from evil" refers to the dangers and trials of earthly life. She contrasts Christ's holy desire for death and the end of His earthly mission with the common human desire to cling to life even in old age.</summary>
<keywords>Our Father, Lord's Prayer, deliverance from evil, Last Supper, desire for God, weariness of life, spiritual trials, mortality</keywords>

For how can he be content, he to whom God has begun to give a taste of something of the felicity of His Kingdom, where one no longer lives by one's own will, but by that of this great and this sovereign Monarch?

432 THE WAY OF PERFECTION Ch. XLII.

If my Confessor <note>Teresa refers here to Father Domingo Bañez, mentioned on the previous page, who was a high-ranking Dominican theologian and her long-time spiritual director.</note>, to whom I will give this writing before you see it, judges that it might be useful to you and places it in your hands, I shall receive no small consolation from the benefit you will derive from it. But if he finds that it is not worthy of being seen, you will be satisfied, if you please, with my good will, since I have obeyed what you commanded me; and I shall consider myself very well rewarded for the trouble I have taken in writing it—I say in *writing* it, having certainly had none in *thinking* of what I had to say <note>Teresa often claimed her writings were divinely inspired or "given" to her, suggesting she acted merely as a vessel for God’s instructions.</note>.

Let us bless and praise forever our Lord, from whom alone proceeds all the good that we think, that we say, and that we do. Amen <note>original: "Ainsi soit-il," the traditional French conclusion for a prayer, meaning "So be it."</note>.
